B routine priorityPublic-health news

Global warming is widening the spread of TBE and chikungunya, vaccine maker says - Pakistan Today

News Brief
The article discusses how global warming is contributing to the spread of chikungunya and tick-borne encephalitis (TBE) into new regions, as stated by Bavarian Nordic CEO Paul Chaplin. He noted that warmer temperatures and milder winters are enabling ticks and mosquitoes to expand their range, leading to increased cases in Germany, Scandinavia, and France. Germany recorded 693 TBE cases in 2025, the third-highest since data collection began in 2001, while France has reported locally acquired chikungunya cases this year. The European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control highlighted that ambient temperature is a critical factor in chikungunya transmission. The situation is under investigation, and the article is a syndicated summary, meaning the full original article body could not be extracted.
Original source text (English)
World Global warming is widening the spread of TBE and chikungunya, vaccine maker says Warmer temperatures and milder winters are helping TBE and chikungunya spread into new regions, according to Bavarian Nordic CEO Paul Chaplin. Germany, Scandinavia, Europe and the US are among the areas cited in the reported shift. News Desk August 22, 2026 1 min read Size Font ISLAMABAD: Rising temperatures and milder winters are helping tick-borne encephalitis and chikungunya reach new areas, according to the chief executive of vaccine manufacturer Bavarian Nordic. Paul Chaplin said on Friday that Germany was already seeing the effects, with the number of areas classified as being at risk of tick-borne encephalitis, or TBE, increasing each year. Speaking to Reuters, he said the disease was also shifting northward from its traditional concentration in southern Germany. Chaplin said warmer conditions and less severe winters were also contributing to the expansion of tick populations in Scandinavia, where TBE cases are increasing as the insects extend their range. Ad powered by advergic.com Germany recorded 693 TBE cases last year, according to the Robert Koch Institute, making it the third-highest annual total since data collection began in 2001. In comments carried by Reuters, Chaplin said: The number of regions in Germany that are now considered to be at risk of TBE increases year after year, He also said chikungunya, a mosquito-borne illness usually associated with Asia and South America, was spreading into Europe and the United States. According to Chaplin, mosquitoes capable of transmitting chikungunya are already present in Europe, creating the possibility that imported infections could lead to local transmission. He pointed to earlier outbreaks in Italy and France as examples. France has reported locally acquired chikungunya cases this year. The European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control has said that ambient temperature is among the most important environmental factors affecting chikungunya transmission. Bavarian Nordic chikungunya vaccine filed for approval in Brazil - scanx.trade

News Brief
Bavarian Nordic has filed for approval of its chikungunya vaccine in Brazil, as reported by ScanX News Team on 13 July 2026. The vaccine, known as CHIKV VLP, is currently under review by Anvisa, Brazil's regulatory authority. The article highlights the anticipated timeline for approval and raises questions about the pricing strategy for the Brazilian market compared to established markets like the US and Europe. It also explores the potential for Eurofarma to expand distribution to other Latin American countries following a successful launch in Brazil. The article notes that Health Canada has already approved the vaccine, which may influence Bavarian Nordic's regulatory strategy in remaining markets in Asia and South America. However, no specific details about the scale of impact, risk levels, or confirmed cases are provided in the evidence. The status of the vaccine's approval remains under investigation.

Chikungunya fever: Themis Bioscience’s vaccine candidate successful in phase 1 - prod.pasteur.ows.fr

Chikungunya fever: Themis Bioscience’s vaccine candidate successful in phase 1 - prod.pasteur.ows.fr
新闻简报Themis Bioscience’s vaccine candidate for chikungunya fever has shown success in phase 1 trials, according to a report from the Institut Pasteur. Published on August 20, 2026, the news highlights the progress in developing a vaccine against the chikungunya virus. The report does not provide specific case counts, death counts, or exposure numbers, but it indicates that the vaccine candidate is effective. The Institut Pasteur is involved in various research initiatives, including the ACT CHIK project aimed at accelerating vaccine development in Africa. The findings from the phase 1 trial are a positive step in the research process, though further details on the vaccine’s current status or next steps are not included in the supplied evidence.

古吉拉特邦2026年基孔肯雅热、登革热和寨卡病例大幅下降

Gujarat records sharp decline in malaria, dengue and chikungunya cases in 2026
新闻简报Gujarat has seen a significant decline in chikungunya cases in 2026, with a 48.1 per cent reduction compared to the same period in 2025. The state reported 80 cases up to epidemiological Week 33, down from 154 in 2025. This decline is attributed to expanded surveillance, testing, and mosquito-control measures, including a house-to-house campaign that covered over 6.51 crore people in its second phase. The state tested more than 12,000 serum samples for chikungunya and has implemented various interventions such as eliminating mosquito breeding sites, fogging in over 3.21 lakh houses, and releasing larvivorous fish at 5,024 permanent waterlogging sites. Officials emphasized the availability of medicines and equipment, and the ongoing monitoring of vector-borne diseases. The measures are particularly important during the monsoon season when stagnant water can create breeding sites for mosquitoes. No deaths due to chikungunya or malaria were recorded during the period, indicating a successful containment effort.
查看新闻五要素
时间
该报告发布于2026年8月19日。数据涵盖2026年第33周(对应至2026年7月)的流行病学数据。
地点与对象
事件发生于印度古吉拉特邦,影响普通人群,特别提及24个地区的疟疾敏感区域和村庄。
事件
Gujarat recorded a decline in chikungunya cases in 2026, with a 48.1 per cent reduction compared to the corresponding period in 2025. The decline is attributed to expanded surveillance, testing, and mosquito-control measures.
规模、影响与风险
基孔肯雅热病例从2025年同期的154例降至2026年第33周的80例。该邦对超过12,000份基孔肯雅热血清样本进行了检测,期间未记录因基孔肯雅热或疟疾导致的死亡。风险正在每日、每周和每月进行监测。
应对、状态与不确定性
The state implemented a house-to-house surveillance programme in two phases, covering over 6.51 crore people. Mosquito breeding sites were eliminated, and fogging was carried out in over 3.21 lakh houses. Biological control measures, such as releasing larvivorous fish, were also used. The measures are part of ongoing efforts to prevent vector-borne diseases during the monsoon season.
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Gujarat records sharp decline in malaria, dengue and chikungunya cases in 2026

News Brief
Gujarat has seen a significant decline in chikungunya cases in 2026, with a 48.1 per cent reduction compared to the same period in 2025. The state reported 80 cases up to epidemiological Week 33, down from 154 in 2025. This decline is attributed to expanded surveillance, testing, and mosquito-control measures, including a house-to-house campaign that covered over 6.51 crore people in its second phase. The state tested more than 12,000 serum samples for chikungunya and has implemented various interventions such as eliminating mosquito breeding sites, fogging in over 3.21 lakh houses, and releasing larvivorous fish at 5,024 permanent waterlogging sites. Officials emphasized the availability of medicines and equipment, and the ongoing monitoring of vector-borne diseases. The measures are particularly important during the monsoon season when stagnant water can create breeding sites for mosquitoes. No deaths due to chikungunya or malaria were recorded during the period, indicating a successful containment effort.
Original source text (English)
SERVING THE COMMUNITY SINCE 1977 Home India World Community Politics Economy Entertainment Legal Startups Sports Life & Style Astrology Health Opinions Classified Business SERVING THE COMMUNITY SINCE 1977 Home India World Community Politics Economy Entertainment Legal Startups Sports Life & Style Astrology Health Opinions Classified Business Home ePaper Categories India World Community Politics Economy Entertainment Legal Startups Sports Life & Style Astrology Health Opinions Classified Business About Us Contact Us Gujarat records sharp decline in malaria, dengue and chikungunya cases in 2026 Home INDIA Gujarat records sharp decline in malaria, dengue and chikungunya cases in 2026 Gujarat records sharp decline in malaria, dengue and chikungunya cases in 2026 by indiatribune August 19, 2026 2 minutes Gujarat records sharp decline in malaria, dengue and chikungunya cases in 2026 (Photo: IANS) Gandhinagar, Aug 19 (IANS) Gujarat has recorded a substantial decline in malaria, dengue and chikungunya cases this year, with the state reporting a 45 per cent fall in malaria, 39.1 per cent in dengue and 48.1 per cent in chikungunya infections up to epidemiological Week 33 compared with the corresponding period in 2025. According to data released by the Gujarat Health Department on World Mosquito Day, malaria cases fell from 1,407 in the corresponding period last year to 774 this year. In contrast, dengue cases declined from 1,293 to 788. Chikungunya cases dropped from 154 to 80. No deaths due to malaria or dengue were recorded during the period. The decline has come alongside expanded surveillance, testing, and mosquito-control measures across the state, including a house-to-house campaign that brought more than 6.51 crore people under surveillance during its second phase. The state tested more than 1.09 crore blood samples for malaria up to Week 33 of 2025, detecting 1,407 cases. In the corresponding period this year, more than 1.11 crore samples were tested, with 774 cases detected. Gujarat has also expanded diagnostic capacity for dengue and chikungunya. Diagnostic centres at 42 hospitals affiliated with medical colleges are testing for both diseases. Up to Week 33 this year, more than 83,000 serum samples for dengue and more than 12,000 for chikungunya had been tested, bringing the combined total to nearly 96,000. The state's house-to-house surveillance programme was conducted in two phases. During the first phase, from April 27 to May 6, the programme achieved 89% coverage through fever surveillance, anti-larval activities, and health education. The second phase, conducted from July 1 to 10, involved 19,558 teams and covered more than 6.51 crore people. The exercise collected more than 2.57 lakh blood samples, identifying 28 malaria patients, all of whom received treatment. The campaign has also focused on eliminating mosquito breeding grounds. House-to-house surveillance eliminated more than 29.63 lakh mosquito breeding sites, and fogging was carried out in more than 3.21 lakh houses. In malaria-sensitive areas, more than one lakh people across 116 villages in 24 districts were covered during the first round of insecticide spraying. The second round began on August 1. To strengthen field-level surveillance during the monsoon, the state deployed 497 vector-control teams across districts and municipal corporations. The teams are conducting fever surveillance and anti-larval activities in areas considered vulnerable to mosquito-borne diseases. The state has also turned to biological control measures, releasing larvivorous fish at 5,024 permanent waterlogging sites across Gujarat by the end of July. The fish target mosquito larvae at breeding sites before they develop into adult mosquitoes. "Medicines, larvicides and essential equipment had been kept adequately available, while the status of vector-borne diseases is being monitored on a daily, weekly and monthly basis," officials said. The measures come during the monsoon, when stagnant water can create breeding sites for mosquitoes. The World Health Organisation notes that many vector-borne diseases can be prevented through protective measures and community mobilisation. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

PMC 今年报告 71 例基孔肯雅热病例,领跑马哈拉施特拉邦各市政机构 - 《印度时报》

PMC leads state’s civic bodies with 71 chikungunya cases - The Times of India
新闻简报《印度时报》报道,今年(2026年1月1日至8月21日),在马哈拉施特拉邦所有市政机构中,浦那市政公司(PMC)报告的基孔肯雅热病例数量最高,共计71例。根据邦卫生部门数据,PMC报告的病例占全邦总计1,096例基孔肯雅热病例的一部分。其中,14例为确诊病例,其余为疑似病例。帕尔加尔县报告病例数最多,为264例,其次为浦那(138例)、拉图尔(56例)和杜莱(55例)。卫生部门正在加强诊断工作并实施预防措施,包括向静止水体投放食蚊鱼以及对蚊虫滋生场所的机构处以罚款。当局还强调早期就医的重要性,并避免自行用药。暴发与浦那大量外来务工人员有关,部分患者来自邻近农村地区。治疗主要表现为症状性,强调早期诊断及消除滋生地是降低并发症的关键。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026年1月1日至8月21日。发布于2026年8月23日。
地点与对象
印度马哈拉施特拉邦浦那。病例报告覆盖全邦,其中浦那市政公司(PMC)报告71例,帕尔加尔县报告病例数最多,为264例。
事件
根据邦卫生部门数据,今年(2026年)PMC在所有市政机构中报告基孔肯雅热病例71例。其中,14例为确诊病例,其余为疑似病例。
规模、影响与风险
2026年1月1日至8月21日期间,全邦共报告基孔肯雅热病例1,096例。PMC报告71例,帕尔加尔县报告病例数最多,为264例。部分患者来自邻近农村地区,32名患者提供的地址信息不完整。卫生部门正在加强诊断工作并实施预防措施。
应对、状态与不确定性
PMC及当地当局在长期高发区加强行动,包括向静止水体投放食蚊鱼、开展宣传并对蚊虫滋生场所的机构处以罚款。响应措施包括重点关注早期诊断及症状性治疗,采用补液和对乙酰氨基酚治疗。患者被建议避免自行用药,若症状持续应及早就医。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

PMC leads state’s civic bodies with 71 chikungunya cases - The Times of India

News Brief
The Times of India reported that Pune Municipal Corporation (PMC) has recorded the highest number of chikungunya cases among all civic bodies in Maharashtra this season, with 71 cases reported between January 1 and August 21, 2026. According to state health department data, PMC's cases are part of a broader state total of 1,096 chikungunya cases. Of these, 14 are confirmed, and the rest are suspected. Palghar district reported the most cases at 264, followed by Pune (138), Latur (56), and Dhule (55). The health department is focusing on diagnosing more cases and implementing preventive measures, such as releasing guppy fish into stagnant water and imposing penalties on institutions with mosquito breeding sites. Authorities also emphasize the importance of early medical attention and avoiding self-medication. The outbreak is linked to the high volume of migrant workers in Pune, and some patients come from neighboring rural areas. While treatment is mainly symptomatic, early diagnosis and eliminating breeding sites are highlighted as key to reducing complications.

Cyber Fraud Case Lucknow Double Murder Maharashtra Hill Incident Kolkata Room Tariffs Reservation Hatao Andolan Sanjay Jadhav Kanpur Cyber Fraud Case PMC leads state’s civic bodies with 71 chikungunya cases Steffy Thevar / Aug 23, 2026, 00:40 IST Comments Share AA +Text Size Small Medium Large Stagnant water in Khilarewadi, Erandwane Pune: PMC has reported the highest number of chikungunya cases among all civic bodies this season, according to the state health department data.Of the 1,096 chikungunya cases reported across the state between Jan 1 and Aug 21 this year, Pune Municipal Corporation (PMC) recorded 71 cases, followed by Mumbai (61), Akola (41), and Kolhapur (34). Among districts, Palghar reported the highest number with 264 cases, followed by Pune (138), Latur (56), and Dhule (55).Dr Rajesh Dighe, assistant health officer, PMC, said, “We have a robust diagnostic system at our sentinel centres, which could be one of the reasons for these higher numbers. Of the 71 reported cases, only 14 are confirmed according to central govt guidelines; the rest are suspected cases. We also have many patients arriving from neighbouring rural areas. Our records show that of the 71 patients, only 29 reside within PMC limits, 12 are from outside the city, and 32 provided incomplete addresses. Nevertheless, we have intensified action in chronic hotspots and are issuing notices to institutions found with mosquito breeding spots.”Dr Aparna Patil, district malaria officer, Pune Zilla Parishad, said, “We are focusing on diagnosing more cases because we do not want to leave any case behind. by Taboolaby Taboola Sponsored LinksSponsored Links Promoted LinksPromoted Links You May Like Brain Fog? Few Know About This Caffeine-Free Daily DrinkClear Mind Learn More Undo We are also focusing on preventive measures like releasing guppy fish into stagnant water, creating awareness, and imposing penalties on institutions found with breeding spots. Additionally, due to the high volume of migrant workers in Pune district, not all cases involve permanent Pune residents.”“Patients must monitor specific symptoms and seek immediate medical attention,” said Dr. Amit Tungenwar, consultant physician at Noble Hospitals and Research Centre. He said, “Chikungunya commonly causes prominent joint pain, often accompanied by fever and skin rashes. Dengue, on the other hand, is typically associated with high fever, severe headache, backache, pain behind the eyes, nausea, vomiting, and reduced appetite. In rare, severe cases, patients may develop bleeding from the gums, nose, or in the stool. Drowsiness, restlessness, or reduced urine output can indicate serious complications involving the brain or kidneys, requiring immediate medical intervention.”Children remain particularly vulnerable to vector-borne infections, according to Dr Nakul Pathak, consultant in paediatric intensive care and emergency at Surya Mother & Child Super Specialty Hospital. He said, “Over the past few weeks, we have witnessed a noticeable rise in both dengue and chikungunya cases due to the intermittent rains. While many patients recover with timely care, some require hospitalisation for persistent high fever, dehydration, low platelet counts, or other severe symptoms.”Dr Vineet Rao, consultant, internal medicine, Jupiter Hospital, said, “Treatment for both illnesses is mainly symptomatic, with adequate hydration and paracetamol for fever and pain forming the cornerstone of care. Early diagnosis, prompt medical attention, and the elimination of mosquito breeding sites remain the most effective measures to reduce complications. Patients should strictly avoid self-medication. The best way to stay protected is simple but consistent: avoid stagnant water around homes, use mosquito repellents and protective clothing, maintain hygiene, and seek medical advice early if a fever persists. Proper monitoring can make a huge difference in recovery.”` You Can Also Check: Gold Rate in Pune | Silver Rate in Pune | Bank Holidays in Pune | Public Holidays in Pune | Petrol Price in Pune | Diesel Price in Pune | CNG Price in Pune | LPG Price in Pune Stay updated with the latest Pune news. Time
Between Jan 1 and Aug 21, 2026. Published on Aug 23, 2026.
Location and population
Pune, Maharashtra, India. Cases reported across the state, with Pune Municipal Corporation (PMC) having 71 cases, and Palghar district reporting the highest with 264 cases.
Event
PMC reported 71 chikungunya cases among all civic bodies this season, according to state health department data. Of these, 14 are confirmed, and the rest are suspected cases.
Scale, impact and risk
A total of 1,096 chikungunya cases were reported across the state between Jan 1 and Aug 21, 2026. PMC recorded 71 cases, with Palghar district having the highest at 264. Some patients arrive from neighboring rural areas, and 32 patients provided incomplete addresses. The health department is focusing on diagnosing more cases and implementing preventive measures.
Response, status and uncertainty
PMC and local authorities have intensified action in chronic hotspots, releasing guppy fish into stagnant water, creating awareness, and imposing penalties on institutions with mosquito breeding spots. The response includes focusing on early diagnosis and symptomatic treatment with hydration and paracetamol. Patients are advised to avoid self-medication and seek medical attention early if symptoms persist.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

全球变暖‘扩大’蜱传脑炎与基孔肯雅热的传播——《快报论坛》

Global warming ‘expands spread’ of TBE, chikungunya - The Express Tribune
新闻简报全球变暖正在扩大蜱传脑炎(TBE)与基孔肯雅热的传播范围,巴伐利亚北方公司首席执行官保罗·查普林表示。气温升高与冬季变暖使蜱虫与蚊虫得以进入新的地区,从而增加德国蜱传脑炎与欧洲及美国基孔肯雅热的风险。德国在2025年报告了693例蜱传脑炎病例,为2001年数据收集以来的第三高发年份;法国今年已报告本地获得性基孔肯雅热病例。欧洲疾病预防与控制中心(ECDC)强调,环境温度是影响基孔肯雅热传播的关键因素。查普林还指出,原发于亚洲与南美洲的基孔肯雅热正在进入欧洲,输入性病例可能引发当地暴发。上述发现基于专家分析与报告数据,未提及具体的防控策略。
查看新闻五要素
时间
全球变暖正在扩大蜱传脑炎(TBE)与基孔肯雅热的地理传播范围,气温升高与冬季变暖使携带病原体的蜱虫与蚊虫得以进入新的地区,巴伐利亚北方公司(该公司生产疫苗)首席执行官保罗·查普林于周五表示。据罗伯特·科赫研究所数据,德国去年报告了693例蜱传脑炎病例,为2001年数据收集以来的第三高发年份。法国今年已报告本地获得性基孔肯雅热病例。
地点与对象
德国与法国是受全球变暖影响、蜱传脑炎与基孔肯雅热传播扩散的国家之一。罗伯特·科赫研究所报告了德国的蜱传脑炎病例,而法国报告了本地获得性基孔肯雅热病例。欧洲疾病预防与控制中心(ECDC)强调了环境温度对基孔肯雅热传播的影响。
事件
巴伐利亚北方公司(该公司生产疫苗)首席执行官保罗·查普林表示,气温升高与冬季变暖使携带病原体的蜱虫与蚊虫得以进入新的地区。德国蜱传脑炎病例有所增加,法国报告了本地获得性基孔肯雅热病例。
规模、影响与风险
欧洲疾病预防与控制中心(ECDC)指出,环境温度是影响基孔肯雅热传播的关键环境因素。蜱传脑炎(TBE)也因蜱虫种群扩张而在斯堪的纳维亚地区呈上升趋势。
应对、状态与不确定性
蜱传脑炎与基孔肯雅热的传播正由专家讨论,保罗·查普林指出蜱传脑炎正从德国南部向北扩散,且欧洲现有蚊虫种群可能引发当地暴发。欧洲疾病预防与控制中心(ECDC)已将环境温度识别为影响基孔肯雅热传播的关键因素。上述发现基于报告数据与专家分析,未显示出具体的应对或防控策略。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Global warming ‘expands spread’ of TBE, chikungunya - The Express Tribune

News Brief
Global warming is expanding the spread of tick-borne encephalitis (TBE) and chikungunya, according to Paul Chaplin, CEO of Bavarian Nordic. Warmer temperatures and milder winters are enabling ticks and mosquitoes to move into new regions, increasing the risk of TBE in Germany and chikungunya in Europe and the United States. Germany reported 693 TBE cases in 2025, the third-highest since data collection began in 2001, while France has seen locally acquired chikungunya cases this year. The European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control (ECDC) highlighted that ambient temperature is a key factor in chikungunya transmission. Chaplin also noted that chikungunya, typically found in Asia and South America, is moving into Europe, with imported cases potentially triggering local outbreaks. These findings are based on expert analysis and reported data, with no specific containment strategy mentioned.
Original source text (English)

Advertisement X Paul Chaplin said Germany was among the countries already experiencing the impact, with the number of regions considered at risk of TBE increasing year after year. “The number of regions in Germany that are now considered to be at risk of TBE increases year after year,” Chaplin told Reuters. Chaplin noted that the disease was moving north from its traditional stronghold in southern Germany. He added TBE was also increasing in Scandinavia as warmer temperatures and milder winters allowed tick populations to grow and expand their range. Germany reported 693 TBE cases last year, the third-highest annual figure since data collection began in 2001, according to the Robert Koch Institute. Chaplin said chikungunya, a mosquito-borne disease typically found in Asia and South America, was also moving into Europe and the United States. Mosquitoes capable of transmitting the virus are already present in Europe, meaning imported cases can trigger local outbreaks, he said, pointing to previous outbreaks in Italy and France. France has reported locally acquired chikungunya cases this year. According to the European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control (ECDC) ambient temperature is among the most important environmental factors influencing the transmission of chikungunya. facebook Time
Global warming is expanding the geographical spread of tick-borne encephalitis (TBE) and chikungunya, with warmer temperatures and milder winters helping disease-carrying ticks and mosquitoes move into new regions, the chief executive of vaccine maker Bavarian Nordic said on Friday. Germany reported 693 TBE cases last year, the third-highest annual figure since data collection began in 2001, according to the Robert Koch Institute. France has reported locally acquired chikungunya cases this year.
Location and population
Germany and France are among the countries experiencing the impact of global warming on the spread of TBE and chikungunya. The Robert Koch Institute reported TBE cases in Germany, while France has reported locally acquired chikungunya cases. The ECDC highlighted the influence of ambient temperature on chikungunya transmission.
Event
The chief executive of vaccine maker Bavarian Nordic, Paul Chaplin, stated that warmer temperatures and milder winters are enabling disease-carrying ticks and mosquitoes to move into new regions. Germany has seen an increase in TBE cases, and France has reported locally acquired chikungunya cases.
Scale, impact and risk
The ECDC noted that ambient temperature is a key environmental factor influencing chikungunya transmission. TBE is also increasing in Scandinavia due to expanding tick populations.
Response, status and uncertainty
The spread of TBE and chikungunya is being discussed by experts, with Paul Chaplin noting the northward movement of TBE from southern Germany and the potential for local outbreaks in Europe due to existing mosquito populations. The ECDC has identified ambient temperature as a critical factor in chikungunya transmission. These findings are based on reported data and expert analysis, with no indication of a specific response or containment strategy.

法国西南部基孔肯雅热病例上升 蚊虫防控升级 - Riviera Radio

Mosquito crackdown as chikungunya cases rise in south-west France - Riviera Radio
新闻简报在法国西南部,由于基孔肯雅热病例上升,蚊虫防控措施已被加强,报告了超过20例本地感染病例。暴发主要集中在吉伦特省和塔恩省,图卢兹附近也有病例报告。在Prignac-et-Marcamps,约1,500人的居民中已出现16例确诊病例。当局已启动灭虫行动,并敦促居民清除积水以消除白纹伊蚊的滋生地。受影响地区的一些户外体育活动已被暂停。目前已在法国83个省份定居的亚洲虎蚊,助长了热带疾病的本地传播。基孔肯雅热虽罕见致命,但可引发突发高热、严重关节和肌肉疼痛、头痛、疲劳和皮疹,关节疼痛可能持续数周或数月。居民被建议穿着长袖衣物、使用驱蚊剂、覆盖储水容器,并定期清空可能积水的物品。当前情况正在积极调查和管理中。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026年8月20日 08:09 | 更新:4天前
地点与对象
法国西南部,吉伦特省和塔恩省,Prignac-et-Marcamps(约1,500人)
事件
法国西南部多地蚊虫防控措施升级,因发现超过20例本地感染基孔肯雅热病例。暴发主要集中在吉伦特省和塔恩省,图卢兹附近也有病例报告。受影响地区的一些户外体育活动已被暂停。
规模、影响与风险
报告超过20例本地感染基孔肯雅热病例。亚洲虎蚊目前已在法国本土83个省份定居,使得原本与热带地区相关的疾病得以在当地传播。基孔肯雅热虽罕见致命,但可引发突发高热、严重关节和肌肉疼痛、头痛、疲劳和皮疹。关节疼痛有时可能持续数周或数月。
应对、状态与不确定性
当局已启动灭虫行动,并要求居民清除积水以消除白纹伊蚊的滋生地。受影响地区的居民被建议穿着长袖衣物、使用驱蚊剂、覆盖储水容器,并定期清空可能积水的物品。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Mosquito crackdown as chikungunya cases rise in south-west France - Riviera Radio

News Brief
In south-west France, mosquito control measures have been intensified due to a rise in chikungunya cases, with over 20 locally acquired infections reported. The outbreaks are primarily concentrated in Gironde and Tarn, with additional cases near Toulouse. In Prignac-et-Marcamps, a population of approximately 1,500 has seen 16 confirmed cases. Authorities have initiated insect-control operations and urged residents to eliminate standing water where tiger mosquitoes breed. Some outdoor sports activities have been suspended in the affected area. The Asian tiger mosquito, now established in 83 French departments, has facilitated the spread of tropical diseases. Chikungunya, while rarely life-threatening, can cause sudden fever, severe joint and muscle pain, headaches, fatigue, and rashes, with joint pain potentially lasting for weeks or months. Residents are advised to wear long clothing, use mosquito repellent, cover water containers, and regularly empty anything that can collect stagnant water. The situation is under active investigation and management.
Original source text (English)

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

法国西南部报告基孔肯雅热和登革热病例

Chikungunya and dengue fever reported in south-west France
新闻简报2026年8月,法国西南部吉伦特省和多尔多涅省(均属新阿基坦大区)报告了基孔肯雅热和登革热病例。这些病例由法国公共卫生部门(Santé Publique France)确认,疑似由虎纹斑蚊传播。由于气温升高,虎纹斑蚊在法国逐渐向北扩散。吉伦特省报告3例本地传播的基孔肯雅热病例,多尔多涅省报告1例登革热病例。整个夏季,该地区共记录28例与旅行相关的基孔肯雅热病例和18例登革热病例。本地传播病例仅占少数,大多数与旅行相关。症状通常较轻,但部分患者可能出现持续性关节痛和严重并发症。报告指出,由于症状轻微,许多病例可能未被诊断。为应对虎纹斑蚊传播风险,已开展灭蚊行动。当前形势仍在调查中,存在本地传播扩散的潜在风险,虎纹斑蚊的存在构成中等风险。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该报告发布于2026-08-21。事件日期为2026年8月18日,整个夏季持续更新。
地点与对象
病例报告于法国西南部,具体为新阿基坦大区的吉伦特省和多尔多涅省。受影响人群包括近期未前往热带地区旅行的个体,以及曾到过流行区的人群。
事件
法国西南部报告基孔肯雅热和登革热病例,包括多尔多涅省和吉伦特省,疑似存在本地传播。病例由法国公共卫生部门(Santé Publique France)确认,疑似由虎纹斑蚊传播。
规模、影响与风险
在8月18日的公报中,吉伦特省报告3例本地传播的基孔肯雅热病例,多尔多涅省报告1例登革热病例。整个夏季,新阿基坦大区共记录28例与旅行相关的基孔肯雅热病例和18例登革热病例。本地传播病例仅占少数,大多数与旅行相关。症状通常较轻,但部分患者可能出现持续性关节痛和严重并发症。由于存在本地传播扩散的潜在风险且虎纹斑蚊存在,风险被评估为中等。
应对、状态与不确定性
法国公共卫生部门(Santé Publique France)已开展灭蚊行动应对报告。本地传播病例仍仅占少数,传播源仍在调查中。报告指出,由于症状轻微,许多病例可能未被诊断。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Chikungunya and dengue fever reported in south-west France

News Brief
In August 2026, cases of chikungunya and dengue fever were reported in south-west France, specifically in the departments of Gironde and Dordogne, which are part of the Nouvelle Aquitaine region. These cases, confirmed by Santé Publique France, are believed to be spread by tiger mosquitoes, which have been gradually moving north in France due to warming temperatures. Three locally transmitted chikungunya cases were reported in Gironde, and one dengue case in Dordogne. Over the summer, 28 travel-related chikungunya and 18 dengue cases were recorded in the region. Local transmission accounts for a minority of cases, with most linked to travel. Symptoms are generally mild, but persistent joint pain and severe complications can occur in some patients. The report highlights that many cases may go undiagnosed due to mild symptoms, and mosquito-killing operations have been conducted in response to the outbreak. The situation remains under investigation, with the potential for local spread and the presence of tiger mosquitoes posing a moderate risk.

Updated: Fri 21 Aug 2026 12:09 CET Share Add a comment Asian tiger mosquitoes can spread potentially serious illnesses including chikungunya and dengue fever. Photo by Martin LELIEVRE / AFP Cases of the tropical diseases of chikungunya and dengue fever have been reported in south-west France, including Dordogne, thought to be spread by tiger mosquitoes. Diseases like chikungunya, dengue and West Nile fever are usually found in people who have visited tropical regions where the illnesses are endemic - but France is seeing an increasing number of cases among people who have no recent travel history. It is believed that the illnesses are being spread by tiger mosquitoes, which have been gradually spreading through France in recent years, due to warming temperatures. The most recent bulletin from Santé Publique France records several cases of transmission autochtone (local transmission) in the Nouvelle Aquitaine region. In the August 18th bulletin, three cases of locally transmitted chikungunya were reported in the Gironde département, which includes Bordeaux, while one case of dengue fever was reported in Dordogne. In Gironde, cases were reported in Saint-Médard-en-Jalles and Prignac-et-Marcamps, while a third case in Talence is believed to be linked to the Prignac-et-Marcamps case. These reports cover only those who went to the doctor and were diagnosed with the illness - in many people the symptoms are mild and may not have required a doctor visit. Advertisement In Dordogne, the dengue fever case was reported in Mouleydier, near Bergerac. Local transmission still accounts for a minority of cases - most of the recorded cases are among those who have recently returned from regions in Africa or Asia where the diseases are endemic. Santé Publique France added that over the course of the summer, 28 cases of travel-related chikungunya were reported in the Nouvelle Aquitaine region, and 18 dengue cases. Local transmission of the illnesses has been linked to tiger mosquitoes - originally from Asia, the mosquitoes have been gradually spreading north through Europe over the last decade, as warming temperatures make the environment more attractive to them. READ ALSO: MAP: Where tiger mosquitoes are found in France Santé Publique France recorded several local mosquito-killing operations after reports of chikungunya and dengue. These illnesses can be serious, but fatalities are very rare in patients who have access to good medical care. Chikungunya - According to French health authorities, in approximately 10 to 40 percent of cases, chikungunya is asymptomatic. Symptoms include a sudden onset of high fever, joint pain that can be intense, muscle pain, headache, and a rash. For most people, the illness runs its course in 10 days with no aftereffects, but in 30 to 40 percent of patients who experience symptoms, it can lead to persistent joint pain. Dengue fever - It is asymptomatic in 50 to 90 percent of cases. For those who are symptomatic, the incubation period on average lasts four to seven days, but it can be three to 14 days, according to Santé Publique France. The 'classic' form involves a high fever, chills, headache, 'retro-orbital' (behind the eyes) pain, vomiting, joint and muscle pain, and occasionally a rash around the fifth day of symptoms. Most of the time, the illness goes away after a few days. However, one to five percent of symptomatic cases can become severe and life-threatening. Time
The report was published on 2026-08-21. The event date is August 18th, 2026, with ongoing updates throughout the summer.
Location and population
The cases were reported in south-west France, specifically in the departments of Gironde and Dordogne, which are part of the Nouvelle Aquitaine region. The affected population includes individuals who have no recent travel history to tropical regions, as well as those who have visited endemic areas.
Event
Cases of chikungunya and dengue fever have been reported in south-west France, including Dordogne and Gironde, with local transmission suspected. The cases are confirmed by Santé Publique France, and the illnesses are believed to be spread by tiger mosquitoes.
Scale, impact and risk
In the August 18th bulletin, three locally transmitted chikungunya cases were reported in Gironde, and one dengue case in Dordogne. Over the summer, 28 travel-related chikungunya and 18 dengue cases were recorded in the Nouvelle Aquitaine region. Local transmission accounts for a minority of cases, with most linked to travel. Symptoms are generally mild, but persistent joint pain and severe complications can occur in some patients. The risk is considered moderate due to the potential for local spread and the presence of tiger mosquitoes.
Response, status and uncertainty
Santé Publique France has conducted mosquito-killing operations in response to the reports. The local transmission is still considered a minority of cases, and the source of the outbreak is under investigation. The report notes that many cases may go undiagnosed due to mild symptoms.

医生呼吁在季风季节警惕登革热、疟疾和基孔肯雅热——印度制药邮报

Doctors urge vigilance against Dengue, Malaria and Chikungunya during monsoon - Indian Pharma Post
新闻简报CARE医院的医生呼吁在季风季节警惕登革热、疟疾和基孔肯雅热,强调及时进行临床评估和监测的必要性。文章指出,登革热患者体温下降并不总是意味着康复,持续症状如全身疼痛、虚弱或异常出血应及时就医。国家虽未报告2024年印度登革热病例和死亡数据(NCVBDC报告印度2024年登革热病例233,519例,死亡297例),但文章未提供基孔肯雅热的具体数据。重点在于提高对症状的认识以及持续开展蚊虫预防工作的重要性,特别是在城市地区,积水可能滋生蚊虫。该文章发布于2026年8月19日,作为公共卫生建议而非确认的流行病学报告。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该文章发布于2026年8月19日。未明确提及事件日期,但上下文指向季风季节,通常在印度发生于6月至9月。
地点与对象
该文章讨论印度的情况,特别提及特伦甘纳邦为报告登革热病例的地区。针对城市地区的一般人群,蚊虫可在积水中滋生。
事件
CARE医院的医生呼吁在季风季节警惕登革热、疟疾和基孔肯雅热,强调及时进行临床评估和监测的重要性。此为公共卫生建议,而非确认的流行病学报告。
规模、影响与风险
国家虽未报告2024年印度登革热病例和死亡数据(NCVBDC报告印度2024年登革热病例233,519例,死亡297例),特伦甘纳邦报告登革热病例10,077例。文章未提供基孔肯雅热的具体病例或死亡数据,但强调若未及时诊断,这些疾病可能导致并发症风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
医生建议每周检查住宅及周边环境,清除积水,并使用驱蚊剂和防护服。文章还强调季风季节过后仍需持续预防工作。该来源为完整原始报道。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Doctors urge vigilance against Dengue, Malaria and Chikungunya during monsoon - Indian Pharma Post

News Brief
Doctors at CARE Hospitals have urged vigilance against dengue, malaria, and chikungunya during the monsoon season, emphasizing the need for timely clinical assessment and monitoring. The article highlights that a decrease in fever does not always indicate recovery in dengue cases, and persistent symptoms like body aches, weakness, or unusual bleeding should prompt medical evaluation. While the National Centre for Vector Borne Diseases Control (NCVBDC) reported 2,33,519 dengue cases and 297 deaths in India in 2024, the article does not provide specific data on chikungunya. The focus is on raising awareness about the symptoms and the importance of continued mosquito prevention efforts, especially in urban areas where stagnant water can breed mosquitoes. The article was published on August 19, 2026, and serves as a public health advisory rather than a confirmed outbreak report.
Original source text (English)
Home News Policy Public Health Digitisation Startup People Interview Opinion Appointment Drug Approval Supply Chain Packaging Biotech Sustainability Events Upcoming Events Past Events Healthcare Hospitals Diagnostic Center Equipment White Papers Home » Healthcare » Doctors urge vigilance against Dengue, Malaria and Chikungunya during monsoon Healthcare Doctors urge vigilance against Dengue, Malaria and Chikungunya during monsoon CARE Hospitals experts highlight that a fall in fever does not necessarily indicate recovery in dengue and stress the importance of timely clinical assessment, appropriate investigations, hydration and monitoring. By IPP Bureau | August 19, 2026 With the monsoon driving a rise in fever cases, doctors at CARE Hospitals have urged people not to dismiss persistent fever and body aches as a routine viral infection, warning that delayed diagnosis of dengue, malaria and chikungunya can increase the risk of complications. According to the National Centre for Vector Borne Diseases Control (NCVBDC), India reported 2,33,519 dengue cases and 297 deaths in 2024, while Telangana recorded 10,077 cases. Dr. Syed Mustafa Ashraf, Senior General Physician, CARE Hospitals, Malakpet, said dengue, malaria and chikungunya can initially present with symptoms similar to viral fever. Persistent fever, worsening weakness, repeated vomiting, severe abdominal pain, unusual bleeding, drowsiness, breathing difficulty or reduced urine output should prompt medical evaluation. Doctors also highlighted that a fall in fever does not necessarily indicate recovery in dengue and stressed the importance of timely clinical assessment, appropriate investigations, hydration and monitoring. Dr. Chaitanya Challa, Senior Consultant – General Medicine, CARE Hospitals, Banjara Hills, said mosquito-borne disease prevention should continue beyond the monsoon. In urban areas, mosquitoes can breed in small amounts of stagnant water found in coolers, flowerpot trays, buckets, tyres, discarded containers, construction sites and uncovered tanks. Doctors recommended inspecting homes and surroundings at least once a week, removing stagnant water, keeping water-storage containers covered and using mosquito repellents, protective clothing and window and door screens. Since Aedesmosquitoes, which transmit dengue, are primarily active during the daytime, daytime protection is also important. Ahead of World Mosquito Day on August 20, CARE Hospitals doctors called for greater awareness of warning signs and cautioned against indiscriminate self-medication for fever, emphasising that mosquito prevention should remain a year-round practice rather than a seasonal exercise. 欧盟疾控中心敦促加强蚊虫控制,欧洲各地疾病风险上升 - INSIGHT EU MONITORING

ECDC urges stronger mosquito control as disease risks rise across Europe - INSIGHT EU MONITORING
新闻简报The European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control (ECDC) has called for stronger mosquito control measures across Europe as the risk of mosquito-borne diseases rises. The report, published on 20 August 2026, highlights the spread of West Nile virus (WNV) and invasive mosquito species, such as the Asian tiger mosquito (Aedes albopictus), which can transmit dengue and chikungunya viruses. As of 13 August 2026, 429 locally acquired WNV cases were reported in nine European countries, with six regions experiencing their first-ever local infections. The ECDC emphasizes the need for integrated mosquito control strategies, including surveillance, community engagement, and targeted solutions. The report also notes the challenges of insecticide resistance and environmental concerns, urging for more diversified control options. While the focus is on WNV, the ECDC also mentions the importance of monitoring other mosquito-borne diseases, including chikungunya, to prevent further spread.
查看新闻五要素
时间
ECDC报告于2026年8月20日发布,事件日期为2026年8月13日,当日欧洲9国报告了429例本地感染的西尼罗病毒感染病例。传播预计将在8月及9月初达到峰值。
地点与对象
报告涵盖欧洲9国,并提及入侵性蚊虫物种在欧洲更多地区的扩散。重点关注欧洲疾病预防控制中心(ECDC)及其监测工作。
事件
ECDC敦促在欧洲各地加强蚊虫控制,因疾病风险上升,报告援引西尼罗病毒(WNV)传播及入侵性蚊虫物种扩散。报告基于ECDC的监测数据及专家陈述。
规模、影响与风险
Six regions in Europe reported locally acquired WNV infections for the first time this year. As of 13 August 2026, 429 locally acquired cases of WNV infection were reported in nine European countries. The risk of mosquito-borne diseases is increasing due to the spread of invasive species and the presence of Culex mosquitoes.
应对、状态与不确定性
ECDC正通过监测、风险评估及实用指导支持欧洲各国。报告强调需加强协调并拓展蚊虫控制解决方案范围。证据基于ECDC每周数据及监测数据,未确认暴发或基孔肯雅病毒的具体病例详情。

ECDC urges stronger mosquito control as disease risks rise across Europe - INSIGHT EU MONITORING

News Brief
The European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control (ECDC) has called for stronger mosquito control measures across Europe as the risk of mosquito-borne diseases rises. Aug 24th, 2026 9:49:38 PM BUSINESS EURO & FINANCE FUNDING LEGAL AFFAIRS SOCIAL AFFAIRS TAXATION TRADE & COMMERCE DIGITALIZATION ARTIFICIAL INTELLIGENCE AUTONOMOUS DRIVING INDUSTRY 4.0 & ROBOTICS INTERNET GOVERNANCE SMART CITIES & REGIONS SECURITY DEFENCE EXTERNAL SECURITY HOME AFFAIRS USA UKRAINE CHINA RUSSIA TÜRKIYE SUSTAINABILITY AGRICULTURE AQUACULTURE CLIMATE ENERGY ENVIRONMENT HEALTH TRANSPORT EVENTS IEU STORE SUBSCRIBE SUBSCRIBE INSIGHT EU FOR AI TRAINING CUSTOMER REQUEST LOGIN ACCOUNT ECDC urges stronger mosquito control as disease risks rise across Europe Aug 20, 2026 Aedes albopictus, chikungunya, Culex mosquitoes, Dengue, disease surveillance, ECDC, invasive mosquitoes, mosquito-borne diseases, public health, West Nile virus Aedes invasive mosquitoes - current known distribution as of April 2026. Source: ECDC/EFSA Stockholm, 20 August 2026 Europe needs to improve its preparedness and control efforts as the risk of mosquito-borne diseases increases, with West Nile virus (WNV) continuing to spread to new regions and invasive mosquito species becoming established in more parts of Europe, the European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control (ECDC) said on World Mosquito Day. This year, six regions in Europe reported locally acquired infections with WNV for the first time ever. According to ECDC’s most recent weekly figures, 429 locally acquired cases of WNV infection have been reported in nine European countries as of 13 August 2026. As transmission usually peaks during August and early September, further cases can be expected. ‘The expansion of West Nile virus transmission as well as the spread of invasive mosquito species demonstrate why Europe needs to strengthen its mosquito-control capacity,’ says Céline Gossner of ECDC. ‘For effective mosquito control, we need close cooperation in Europe and an integrated approach that brings together mosquito monitoring, community engagement, and targeted mosquito control solutions that take environmental impact into account.’ While the mosquitoes that spread WNV (Culex mosquitoes) are already widely present in Europe, the Asian tiger mosquito (Aedes albopictus), which can transmit dengue and chikungunya viruses, is now established in 16 European countries, double the number from 12 years ago. A new article published today by ECDC and EU partners in the Eurosurveillance journal, ‘Strengthening mosquito control to manage emerging mosquito-borne diseases’, identifies gaps in Europe’s mosquito-control capacity and calls for improved coordination and a bigger toolbox to increase mosquito control. This coincides with the publication of a new ECDC report on mosquito surveillance and control practices in Europe, highlighting the need for more effective mosquito-control programmes. ECDC works to support European countries through seasonal surveillance of WNV infections, dengue and chikungunya virus disease, alongside risk assessments, mosquito-distribution maps, modelling and practical guidance on preparedness, prevention and outbreak response. Effective mosquito control requires an integrated approach that combines mosquito and disease surveillance, larval source management, community engagement, and targeted mosquito control adapted to local conditions. Insecticide resistance, environmental concerns, and the limited range of products authorised for use against mosquitoes also underline the need for more diversified control options and stronger evidence on their effectiveness, safety, and environmental impact. By removing or covering stagnant water in and around homes, including balconies and gardens, communities and individuals have an important role to play in helping to reduce mosquito populations. Personal protection is also important, including using repellents, wearing protective clothing and mosquito-proofing homes. Healthcare professionals should also remain alert to local transmission and the possibility of importation to prevent the further spread of these viruses. 2026年世界蚊子日:欧盟科学家敦促加强蚊虫控制,应对疾病上升趋势 - Bioengineer.org

World Mosquito Day 2026: EU scientists urge mosquito control as diseases rise - Bioengineer.org
新闻简报2026年8月21日,来自欧洲疾病预防控制中心(ECDC)、欧洲化学品管理局(ECHA)、欧洲环境署(EEA)及欧盟委员会的欧盟科学家敦促加强蚊虫控制措施,因包括基孔肯雅热、登革热和西尼罗河病毒病在内的蚊媒疾病在欧洲已成为日益严峻的风险。该评论文章于2026年8月20日发表在《欧洲监测》期刊上,将这些疾病的传播与环境变化、气候变化及国际旅行增加联系起来。文章强调需采用可持续的、循证策略来控制蚊虫种群并降低疾病传播风险。文章指出,蚊虫控制固然重要,但其有效性取决于当地生态环境、实施时机、覆盖范围及重复监测。同时,文章还指出碎片化治理带来的挑战,并强调跨境协调的重要性。作者强调,即使公共当局实施控制计划,个人防护措施(如驱蚊剂和蚊帐)仍然重要。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该文章发布于2026年8月21日。文章讨论的事件日期为2026年8月24日,因其涉及蚊媒疾病在欧洲成为日益严峻风险的影响。
地点与对象
该事件与欧洲相关,欧洲疾病预防控制中心(ECDC)、欧洲化学品管理局(ECHA)、欧洲环境署(EEA)及欧盟委员会的科学家敦促加强蚊虫控制措施。受影响人群包括面临基孔肯雅热、登革热和西尼罗河病毒病等蚊媒疾病风险的个体。
事件
欧盟科学家(包括ECDC、ECHA、EEA及欧盟委员会的专家)敦促加强并协调蚊虫控制措施,因基孔肯雅热、登革热和西尼罗河病毒病等蚊媒疾病在欧洲已成为日益严峻的风险。该呼吁基于2026年8月20日发表在《欧洲监测》期刊上的评论文章。
规模、影响与风险
评论文章强调蚊媒疾病传播模式的变化,将其传播与环境变化、气候变化及国际旅行增加联系起来。文章强调需采用可持续的、循证策略来控制蚊虫种群并降低疾病传播的概率。文中指出,由于上述因素,风险正在上升,但报道中未提供具体病例数、死亡人数或暴露人数的数据。
应对、状态与不确定性
响应措施包括蚊虫控制策略,如清除积水、使用杀幼虫剂及实施不育昆虫技术(SIT)。这些干预措施被描述为预防暴发的关键。然而,这些措施的有效性取决于当地生态环境、实施时机、覆盖范围、应用方法及重复监测。评论文章还指出,关于成本效益的证据存在缺口,并强调跨境协调的必要性。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

World Mosquito Day 2026: EU scientists urge mosquito control as diseases rise - Bioengineer.org

News Brief
On August 21, 2026, EU scientists from the European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control (ECDC), the European Chemicals Agency (ECHA), the European Environment Agency (EEA), and the European Commission urged stronger mosquito control measures as mosquito-borne diseases like chikungunya, dengue, and West Nile virus become increasingly established risks in Europe. The commentary, published in Eurosurveillance on August 20, 2026, links the spread of these diseases to environmental changes, climate change, and increased international travel. It emphasizes the need for sustainable, evidence-based strategies to manage mosquito populations and reduce disease transmission. The article highlights that while mosquito control is essential, its effectiveness depends on local ecology, timing, coverage, and repeated monitoring. It also notes the challenges of fragmented governance and the importance of cross-border coordination. The authors stress that personal protection measures, such as repellents and mosquito nets, remain important even when public authorities implement control programs.
Original source text (English)
HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Monday, August 24, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Health World Mosquito Day 2026: EU scientists urge mosquito control as diseases rise by Bioengineer August 21, 2026 in Health Reading Time: 5 mins read 0 Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram Mosquito control must become a stronger and more coordinated public-health priority as dengue, chikungunya and West Nile virus infection become increasingly established risks in Europe, according to scientists from the European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control (ECDC), the European Chemicals Agency (ECHA), the European Environment Agency (EEA) and the European Commission. In a commentary published in Eurosurveillance on 20 August 2026, the authors argue that Europe needs sustainable, evidence-based and integrated strategies to manage mosquito populations and reduce the probability that infectious diseases will spread. Their assessment, published on World Mosquito Day, links the changing epidemiology of mosquito-borne disease to a combination of environmental transformation, climate change and increased international movement of people. Mosquitoes are biological vectors: they acquire viruses when feeding on an infected host and can later transmit those viruses through subsequent bites. The capacity of a mosquito population to sustain transmission depends on several interacting factors, including the presence of competent mosquito species, temperature, rainfall, breeding habitats, virus introduction and the availability of humans or animals that can serve as hosts. Warmer conditions can accelerate viral replication inside mosquitoes and shorten the time required for them to become infectious, while changes in rainfall and water management can create new larval habitats. More frequent international travel can also introduce viruses into areas where mosquito populations are present but local transmission has previously been limited or absent. Discover more infections Pest Control infection Health The European disease landscape has already demonstrated why this issue requires sustained attention. Aedes mosquitoes, which can transmit dengue and chikungunya viruses, have expanded their presence in parts of Europe, while Culex mosquitoes are important vectors of West Nile virus. The establishment of a vector does not automatically lead to an outbreak, because transmission also depends on virus circulation, weather, human behaviour and public-health preparedness. Nevertheless, when suitable mosquitoes and favourable environmental conditions coincide, imported infections can lead to local transmission. The authors therefore describe mosquito control as an essential component of outbreak prevention rather than merely a response used after disease transmission has begun. The most direct way to reduce mosquito abundance is to eliminate or manage the stagnant water in which larvae develop. Containers, rainwater collection points, blocked drainage systems and other small pools can provide breeding sites for Aedes mosquitoes, while larger wetlands, irrigation structures and urban water systems may support other species. Where water cannot be removed, larvicides can be used to target immature mosquitoes before they emerge as adults capable of biting and transmitting pathogens. During outbreaks, adult mosquitoes may also be targeted with biocidal products. These interventions can reduce vector density rapidly, but their performance depends on local ecology, timing, coverage, application methods and repeated monitoring. Another approach now being piloted in Europe is the sterile insect technique, or SIT. This method involves mass-rearing male mosquitoes, sterilising them and releasing them into areas where they can mate with wild females. Because the resulting eggs are infertile, repeated releases can reduce the reproductive capacity of the target population. Male mosquitoes do not bite, so the technique is designed to suppress populations without increasing the number of biting insects. SIT may offer a valuable alternative or complement to chemical control, but its effectiveness depends on accurate releases, adequate population coverage and detailed knowledge of mosquito ecology. It also requires assessment of operational costs, long-term sustainability and effects under different environmental conditions. Discover more Infectious Diseases health Insects & Entomology Chemical control remains important, yet it presents a growing scientific and regulatory challenge. Pyrethroids account for most of the substances approved for adult mosquito control and can interfere with the normal function of insect nervous systems, producing rapid knockdown or death. Their extensive use, however, raises concerns about possible effects on human health and non-target organisms, particularly when applications become broader or more frequent. Mosquito resistance is an additional threat. Genetic changes affecting insecticide target sites, increased detoxification of active compounds or behavioural changes can allow mosquitoes to survive exposure. As resistance increases, products may become less effective, forcing programmes to improve surveillance, rotate control methods and avoid unnecessary applications. The commentary identifies major gaps in the evidence needed to decide which interventions should be used, where and at what scale. Reliable comparisons of cost-effectiveness remain limited, especially for programmes that combine larval control, adult control, environmental management, personal protection and emerging technologies. A measure that works in one setting may perform poorly elsewhere because mosquito species, housing, infrastructure, climate and community behaviour differ. Countries also vary in the biocidal products they approve and in the rules governing their use. These differences can complicate cross-border responses, procurement and the rapid deployment of control measures when vectors or viruses move across national boundaries. Personal protection remains an important layer of prevention, even when public authorities are operating mosquito-control programmes. Repellents can reduce the likelihood of bites, while long clothing, window and door screens, and mosquito nets can create physical barriers between people and vectors. Such measures are particularly important for individuals infected with a mosquito-borne virus, because preventing additional bites can reduce the opportunity for mosquitoes to acquire the virus and pass it to others. Personal protection cannot replace population-level control, but it can reduce exposure while surveillance teams investigate transmission and authorities apply targeted interventions. The authors argue that fragmented governance is one of the greatest obstacles to effective mosquito control. Responsibility commonly rests with ministries of health, although municipal authorities may lead local operations, and ministries responsible for agriculture, the environment, transport or urban development may also influence mosquito habitats and disease risk. Control plans are frequently designed below the national level and implemented by private contractors, creating potential differences in capacity and performance. ECDC surveys conducted in 2020 and 2025 found that 20 of 29 countries reported active mosquito-control measures, with most focusing on larvae. However, the absence of a political perception that mosquito-borne diseases represent an immediate priority can limit investment in surveillance, laboratory capacity, trained personnel and long-term prevention. At the European level, the response involves institutions with different but complementary responsibilities. ECDC and the European Food Safety Authority provide scientific guidance and surveillance expertise for human and animal health, while ECHA evaluates biocidal substances and products used for mosquito control. The EEA contributes environmental and climate information that can help identify changing conditions and assess risk, and the European Commission supports the implementation of health legislation, vector surveillance, public-health measures, coordination and research funding. The scientists call for these activities to be connected through a One Health framework that includes public health, veterinary medicine, environmental protection and urban planning. By combining information on human cases, animal infections, mosquito abundance, insecticide resistance, climate and land use, Europe could detect emerging risks earlier and select interventions that reduce disease transmission while protecting ecosystems and communities. Scale, impact and risk
The commentary highlights the changing epidemiology of mosquito-borne diseases, linking their spread to environmental changes, climate change, and increased international travel. It emphasizes the need for sustainable, evidence-based strategies to manage mosquito populations and reduce the probability of disease transmission. The risk is described as increasing due to these factors, but no specific case counts, death counts, or exposure numbers are reported in the evidence.
Response, status and uncertainty
The response involves mosquito control strategies, including eliminating stagnant water, using larvicides, and implementing the sterile insect technique (SIT). These interventions are described as essential for outbreak prevention. However, the effectiveness of these measures depends on local ecology, timing, coverage, application methods, and repeated monitoring. The commentary also notes gaps in evidence for cost-effectiveness and the need for cross-border coordination.

扎舍娃博士告诉FAKTI:我国境内虎纹蚊的存在并不意味着它们已感染疾病 - fakti.bg

Dr Anelia Zasheva tells FAKTI: The presence of tiger mosquitoes in our country does not mean that they are infected - fakti.bg

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Dr Anelia Zasheva tells FAKTI: The presence of tiger mosquitoes in our country does not mean that they are infected - fakti.bg

News Brief
The article discusses the presence of tiger mosquitoes (Aedes albopictus) in Bulgaria and clarifies that their existence does not automatically mean they are infected with diseases like chikungunya, dengue, or Zika. Dr. Anelia Zasheva, an infectious diseases specialist, explains that while these viruses can be transmitted by mosquitoes, the risk of local outbreaks in Bulgaria is not yet confirmed. She highlights that the spread of these diseases depends on a complex chain of events, including an infected traveler, suitable temperatures for viral multiplication in the mosquito, and subsequent bites to others. The article also notes that the presence of tiger mosquitoes is a necessary but not sufficient condition for local transmission. It emphasizes the importance of monitoring mosquito activity and being aware of symptoms, especially after travel to regions where these diseases are endemic. The risk remains uncertain and contingent on multiple factors, including climate change and the behavior of the mosquito population.
Original source text (English)
Mosquitoes are not just an annoying summer problem. They can be carriers of serious infections, some of which are already appearing in Europe under conditions that were previously less common. West Nile fever deserves special attention, since a large part of those infected have no symptoms and may not even realize that they have had the infection. Against the backdrop of higher temperatures, longer warm seasons and the spread of the Asian tiger mosquito in Bulgaria, prevention is becoming increasingly important. What are the real risks, which symptoms should not be underestimated and how can we protect ourselves from diseases transmitted by mosquitoes... Dr. Anelia Zasheva, an infectious diseases specialist at the Military Medical Academy, spoke to FACTI. - Dr. Zasheva, what are the most dangerous infections that can be transmitted through a mosquito bite, and which of them pose a real risk to people in Bulgaria? - When we talk about the “most dangerous” infections, it is necessary to distinguish the global burden of diseases from the real risk to Bulgaria. Worldwide, malaria remains the leading cause of mortality among mosquito-borne infections. Yellow fever, severe dengue, which usually develops with repeated illness, Japanese encephalitis and the neuroinvasive form of West Nile infection can be particularly serious and life-threatening. Chikungunya is rarely fatal, but it can cause prolonged and severe joint pain, and Zika infection is particularly dangerous during pregnancy due to the risks to the fetus. The immediate risk for people in Bulgaria is West Nile infection. Its main vectors are mosquitoes of the genus Culex, and the virus circulates between mosquitoes and birds. Malaria, dengue, chikungunya and Zika are currently mainly associated with travel. The establishment of the Asian tiger mosquito creates the possibility of an imported virus being transmitted locally, but this is a potential risk. - West Nile fever is among the diseases that are already occurring in our country. Why do about 80% of infected people have no symptoms and how does this make it difficult to detect the infection? - There is no single reason why one person remains asymptomatic and another becomes ill. The clinical outcome depends on the interaction between the virus and the body – age, immune response, concomitant diseases, individual genetic characteristics, viral strain and probably the amount of virus at the time of infection. In most people, the immune system limits the multiplication of the virus before a clinically apparent disease develops. According to various epidemiological and clinical studies, about 20% of people develop symptoms such as fever, headache, fatigue, muscle pain, sometimes rash, nausea or enlarged lymph nodes. These symptoms are non-specific and can easily be attributed to another viral infection, especially in the summer season. Therefore, the registered cases do not represent the total spread of the infection. The real circulation is estimated not only by diagnosed patients, but also by examining blood donors, mosquitoes, birds and horses. - What are the first symptoms that should make us seek medical help after a mosquito bite – especially if we have been abroad or in an area with established circulation of the infection? - It is not necessary to seek medical attention after every bite. The usual redness, itching and slight swelling are a local reaction to the mosquito's saliva and most often pass without complications. A medical consultation is necessary if in the following days or weeks a fever, severe headache, severe fatigue, muscle or joint pain, pain behind the eyes, rash, nausea or vomiting occur. Immediate help should be sought in case of confusion, drowsiness, neck stiffness, seizures, sudden muscle weakness or paralysis, difficulty breathing, persistent vomiting, severe abdominal pain, bleeding or fainting. A particularly important rule is that any fever during or after traveling to an area with malaria should be evaluated urgently - even if the person has taken prophylaxis. The doctor should be informed exactly where, when and for how long the patient has traveled, as well as what prophylaxis he has taken. If dengue is suspected, aspirin and nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs should not be taken arbitrarily until the diagnosis has been ruled out, as they can increase the risk of bleeding. - How serious can the complications of West Nile infection be and which people are most at risk of severe nervous system damage? - Approximately one in 150 infected people develops neuroinvasive disease - meningitis, encephalitis or involvement of the anterior horns of the spinal cord, which can resemble poliomyelitis and cause acute flaccid paralysis. In the most severe cases, the respiratory muscles may also be affected. It is important to note that the severe form is rare compared to all diagnosed cases, but when it develops, it can be life-threatening. Among patients with neuroinvasive disease, the mortality rate in some studies and seasons reaches 20%, and some of those who have recovered may have prolonged weakness, memory and concentration disorders, tremors or other neurological consequences. The risk increases with age, especially in people over 60-65 years of age. People with a suppressed immune system, transplant patients and patients with oncological diseases, diabetes, chronic kidney disease or arterial hypertension are particularly at risk. However, severe cases have also been recorded in young people. There is still no approved vaccine or specific antiviral treatment. - The Asian tiger mosquito already has established populations in Bulgaria. How can we recognize it and does its presence mean that there is already a real risk of dengue, chikungunya and Zika in our country? - The Asian tiger mosquito – Aedes albopictus – is relatively small and dark. It has clearly defined white or silvery rings on its legs and a single light longitudinal line on the upper side of the thorax. Unlike many common mosquitoes, it bites aggressively mainly during the day, most often outdoors. However, a photo alone cannot always reliably distinguish it from other similar species. Observations can also be submitted through the NCCSPB Mosquito Alert project. Its presence is a necessary, but not sufficient condition for the local spread of dengue, chikungunya or Zika. It takes an infected traveler to arrive in Bulgaria while the virus is circulating in their bloodstream; a local mosquito to bite them; the temperature to allow the virus to multiply in the mosquito; and then the same mosquito to bite another person. Therefore, the risk is possible, but it depends on many factors. The presence of tiger mosquitoes in our country does not mean that they are infected or that these diseases are already endemic. Local outbreaks of dengue and chikungunya in other European countries show that such a chain of transmission is possible. For Zika, Aedes albopictus is considered a less efficient vector compared to Aedes aegypti. - How climate change – higher temperatures, longer summers and milder winters – change the spread of mosquitoes and the infections they carry? - Higher temperatures accelerate the development of mosquitoes and, under the right conditions, shorten the time it takes for the virus to become infectious in the mosquito itself. Longer summers extend the transmission season, and milder winters facilitate the survival of eggs and the establishment of invasive species. Precipitation also matters. Rain creates water habitats, but periods of drought can also increase the risk when people start storing water in open containers. The relationship is not completely linear - extreme heat and prolonged drought can also reduce some populations. - Could Bulgaria become a more suitable environment for these diseases? - Bulgaria can become, and to some extent has already become, more suitable for certain mosquitoes and infections. This is evidenced by the establishment of the tiger mosquito and the periodic circulation of the West Nile virus. But climate is not the only factor. International travel, urbanization, water management, vector surveillance and timely control also determine whether an outbreak will occur. Therefore, a more favorable environment does not mean that Bulgaria will inevitably become a tropical endemic zone. - What should people who travel to countries with widespread malaria, dengue, yellow fever or other infections know? When is a vaccine needed, and when is drug prophylaxis needed? - It is best to consult with an infectious disease specialist four to eight weeks before departure. Not only the country is evaluated, but also the specific region, season, altitude, duration and mode of travel, as well as the age, presence of pregnancy, concomitant diseases and immune status of the traveler. Even when traveling at the last minute, consultation makes sense. A vaccine is used for yellow fever, which is recommended for some countries, and for others the certificate is a condition for entry. One dose provides long-lasting, usually lifelong protection, but the vaccine has contraindications and requires individual assessment, especially in immunosuppressed, pregnant women, young children and people over 60 years of age. For travelers to malarial areas, the main specific method is drug prophylaxis. The choice between the different drugs depends on local resistance, contraindications and the route. They are started before the trip and continued during the stay and a certain period after return, which varies for the individual drugs. There is no drug prophylaxis for dengue. There is a vaccine, but it is not a universal recommendation for every tourist and its use depends on age, data on previous infection, length of stay and current national recommendations. Neither the vaccine nor drug prophylaxis replaces protection from bites: repellent, covering clothing, nets, air-conditioned rooms and appropriately treated nets or clothing. - Can we distinguish a simple mosquito bite from a situation in which we are likely to be infected with a virus or parasite? And does it make sense for a person to test their blood prophylactically after a bite? - No. An infected mosquito does not look different, and the bite itself does not indicate whether the pathogen has been transmitted. Itching, redness and swelling are a reaction to the mosquito's saliva, not to the virus or parasite. Prophylactic testing after a single bite in a person without symptoms usually has no medical meaning. If done too early, the test may be negative despite the infection having occurred. On the other hand, some antibody tests may cross-react with other similar viruses or indicate an old, rather than a current infection. Tests are ordered when there is a combination of symptoms, an appropriate incubation period, and actual epidemiological exposure. Exceptions may include special recommendations for pregnant women after possible Zika exposure, measures for blood donation, or specific instructions from health authorities. - What are the most effective measures to limit mosquitoes around our homes? How important is it to remove stagnant water and why can even small containers become mosquito breeding grounds? - Removing stagnant water is one of the most effective measures, especially against the tiger mosquito. Once a week, we should empty, wash and scrub or turn over all containers that can hold water: pot bases, buckets, watering cans, children's toys, vases, tires, gutters, covers, birdbaths and pet bowls. It is important not only to pour out the water, but also to scrub the walls. The tiger mosquito lays desiccation-resistant eggs just above the waterline, and these can remain attached to the container until it is flooded again. The larvae do not need a swamp for development - a small amount of water in a forgotten container is enough. Rainwater tanks should be tightly covered, pools - properly maintained, and window and door screens - strong. When water cannot be removed, only approved larvicides can be used and exactly as directed. Spraying against adult mosquitoes has a temporary effect and cannot replace the removal of breeding sites. - What is the most important message to people during the summer season – what should we really be afraid of and what can we do to reduce the risk of infections without becoming unnecessarily anxious? - We should not be afraid of every bite. For the vast majority of people, it will only cause short-term itching and redness. But we should not underestimate the fact that conditions in Europe are changing and some mosquito-borne infections are now a real, albeit relatively rare, possibility. The most sensible approach is to be informed without panicking: to use repellent and appropriate clothing, to limit areas with stagnant water and to put nets on windows. If you have a fever or unusual symptoms after traveling, you should seek medical attention and always report where you were and for how long. It is not the mosquito itself that should scare us, but the ignored fever after a risky trip, the missed warning symptoms, and the lack of basic prevention. Most risks can be minimized with a few consistent and completely doable habits

沃尔巴克氏体如何成为对抗疾病媒介的盟友——《科学人》

How Wolbachia Emerged as an Ally in the Fight Against Disease Vectors - the-scientist.com
新闻简报本文讨论了利用沃尔巴克氏体(一种细菌内共生体)控制疾病媒介的方法。研究人员通过感染蚊子来对抗登革热和基孔肯雅热等蚊媒病毒,这种方法可降低蚊子传播这些病毒的能力。沃尔巴克氏体还可用于靶向寄生性丝虫,研究显示抑制沃尔巴克氏体生长可破坏丝虫的存活。巴西的世界蚊子计划已将沃尔巴克氏体感染蚊子作为一种生物防控策略。此外,公民科学项目正在帮助绘制沃尔巴克氏体在多种昆虫物种中的分布图。本文强调了对沃尔巴克氏体抗病毒药物机制的持续研究及其在公共卫生领域的更广泛应用。这些努力旨在通过改变蚊子生物学特性及靶向病原体微生物组来减少疾病传播。
