B routine priorityPublic-health news

How Wolbachia Emerged as an Ally in the Fight Against Disease Vectors - the-scientist.com

News Brief
The article discusses the use of Wolbachia, a bacterial endosymbiont, in controlling disease vectors. Researchers have leveraged Wolbachia to combat mosquito-borne viruses like dengue and chikungunya by infecting mosquitoes, which reduces their ability to transmit these viruses. The bacteria also play a role in targeting parasitic filarial worms, with studies showing that inhibiting Wolbachia's growth can disrupt the worms' survival. The World Mosquito Program in Brazil has been using Wolbachia-infected mosquitoes as a biocontrol strategy. Additionally, citizen science projects are helping to map Wolbachia's presence in various insect species. The article highlights the ongoing research into Wolbachia's antiviral mechanisms and its broader applications in public health. These efforts aim to reduce the spread of diseases by modifying mosquito biology and targeting the microbiome of pathogens.
Original source text (English)
Skip to main content Login Register News Magazines An Interactive Experience Current Issue August 2026 View this Issue Archive Quarterly Magazine Current Issue June 2026 View this Issue ArchiveFeatures Topics Life Sciences Biochemistry Cancer Cell & Molecular Biology Developmental Biology Evolutionary Biology Genetics Genome Editing Immunology Microbiology Neuroscience Omics Physiology Health & Medicine Cell & Gene Therapy Diagnostics Drug Discovery & Development Public Health Science & Society Community Careers Research Ethics Science Communication Biotechnology Artificial Intelligence Business Laboratory Technology Synthetic Biology Resources Crossword Puzzles eBooks Infographics Innovation Spotlight Podcasts Product Coverage Research Product Blog Industry Articles Videos Words for Nerds Webinars Register Home/ Categories/ Life Sciences/ Microbiology Article How Wolbachia Emerged as an Ally in the Fight Against Disease Vectors From blocking viral replication to suppressing pathogenic worms, Wolbachia’s unusual relationships with its hosts inspire new ways to control disease spread. Brought to you byThe Scientist Aug 24, 2026 | 4 min read Save for Later Researchers leverage Wolbachia to combat mosquito-borne viruses, target parasitic worms, and engage citizen scientists. Image credit:© iStock.com, iiievgeniy PDF version Register for free to listen to this article Listen with Speechify 0:00 4:00 Discovered more than a century ago in mosquitoes, the bacterial genus Wolbachia is one of the most common parasitic microbes of insects. The bacteria reside in the cytoplasm of host cells and modify their reproductive capabilities, often resulting in sterility. Over the past 100 years, this endosymbiont has captivated researchers’ interest, both due to its interaction with its host and because of its applications in controlling disease vectors. Dive in to revisit the endosymbiont’s journey, right from its discovery to how researchers leverage it to control vector-borne viruses like dengue, target the obligate bacteria inside parasitic filarial worms, and engage citizen scientists globally to map its vast biological distribution. A Century of Wolbachia: From Discovery to Controlling Diseases In the early 1900s, entomologist Marshall Hertig and pathologist Simeon Burt Wolbach brought mosquitoes to their lab to characterize the microbes they harbored. Dissecting insect tissues and examining them under the microscope revealed bacteria in their ovaries, testes, and eggs. Over time, the researchers found these endosymbionts in a number of insect species and identified that infected female insects passed Wolbachia to their offspring. Digging deeper, researchers discovered that Wolbachia manipulated its host reproductive success, turning the bacterium into an unlikely ally in the fight against pathogens. Wolbachia (red) are known to infect the oocytes (blue and green) of insects such as Drosophila melanogaster. Yonah Radousky Wolbitos Help Control Disease Vectors in the Field A few years ago, vector biologist Luciano Moreira joined forces with University of Queensland biologist and founder and chief executive officer of the World Mosquito Program Scott O’Neill to leverage Wolbachia as a biocontrol agent against mosquito-borne pathogens. Building on previous work indicating that Wolbachia could induce resistance to viral pathogens in their hosts, the duo infected Aedes aegypti mosquitoes with the endosymbiont and exposed them to dengue and chikungunya viruses. Compared to uninfected mosquitoes, Wolbachia-infected mosquitoes had lower levels of viral genetic materials. These insects also had an impaired ability to obtain blood meals, which could reduce their disease transmitting ability. Encouraged by this, Moreira pioneered the use of Wolbachia-infected mosquitoes, later nicknamed “wolbitos,” to control disease spread in Brazil as part of the World Mosquito Program there. Continue reading below... Like this story? Sign up for FREE Immunology updates: Latest science news storiesTopic-tailored resources and eventsCustomized newsletter content Subscribe Wolbachia as Filarial Worms’ Achilles Heel When Institute for Advanced Biosciences chemist Andrés Palencia was developing therapies to target roundworms that cause lymphatic filariasis, a devastating parasitic disease, he knew he needed to get creative. He turned to knowledge from the 1970s, when scientists discovered that microscopic roundworms that spread the disease depend on Wolbachia for their growth and survival. Palencia and his team leveraged this to develop a drug that inhibits Wolbachia’s growth, disrupting its symbiosis with its nematode host and highlighting how targeting a pathogen’s microbiome could be a promising approach to control infections in humans. Researchers are producing Wolbachia-infected A. aegypti mosquitoes as a strategy to control the spread of dengue virus in many locations around the world. Flavio Carvalho, World Mosquito Program Brazil An Animal Biotechnologist Uses Sterile Insect Technique to Control Disease-Causing Vectors As part of her PhD research, Asha Wijegunawardana, now an animal biotechnologist at the Rajarata University of Sri Lanka, studied population control methods for mosquitoes carrying the dengue virus. She explored both sterilization of male mosquitoes and infecting female insects with Wolbachia, which blocks the insects’ ability to carry the virus. Screening native mosquito species revealed that the majority did not carry Wolbachia, offering a promising approach to leverage this endosymbiont to control dengue virus-transmitting vectors. Her team also uses genetic modification approaches to reduce the reproductive and pathogen-spreading abilities of other insects such as sandflies, which spread the parasite that causes leishmaniasis. Wolbachia Brings Citizen Scientists Together Nearly 20 years ago, Pennsylvania State University biologist Seth Bordenstein and his colleagues launched a citizen science project called Discover the Microbes Within! The Wolbachia Project. The goal was to screen insects and help researchers identify new hosts that harbor Wolbachia, which had been challenging given that nearly half of the seven million insect species on Earth carry the Wolbachia endosymbiont. Citizen scientists collect and identify insects, carrying out PCR using reagents and equipment provided by the program to investigate whether the arthropods harbor Wolbachia. They can also send the samples for sequencing. Through this project, participants have assessed the presence of Wolbachia in 1,500 insect species and shared their findings in the project’s user database. How Wolbachia-Infected Mosquitoes Prevent Dengue Virus Replication While researchers established that Wolbachia-infected mosquitoes act as biocontrol agents to keep dengue at bay, they did not completely understand the underlying mechanism. Robson Loterio, a microbiologist at the Burnet Institute sought to find out. Subjecting Wolbachia-infected mosquitoes to microscopy revealed that the endosymbionts largely clustered near the host cells’ endoplasmic reticulum. Since this organelle produces molecules used in lipid synthesis, the researchers dug deeper and found that Wolbachia infection—which prevented dengue viral replication—led to lipid accumulation in cells. Inhibiting this lipid buildup allowed dengue virus replication, indicating that Wolbachia’s antiviral mechanism relies on excess lipid aggregation. Releasing Millions of Mosquitoes to Help Curb West Nile Virus As part of the Google Debug project, researchers infected West Nile virus-spreading male mosquitoes with a strain of Wolbachia that renders its host sterile. When these mosquitoes mate with an uninfected female mosquito, the mating is incompatible and the eggs do not hatch. By combining automated rearing systems and AI-driven approaches to sort males from females, the Debug project strategy aims to release only Wolbachia-carrying male mosquitoes, which is safer for people since male mosquitoes do not bite. Its Atmospheric Control Unit (ACU) enables precise regulation of oxygen and carbon dioxide levels inside the reader, creating an ideal environment for live cell-based assays. This feature is critical for experiments that require physiological conditions, such as cytotoxicity studies, metabolic assays, and long-term kinetic measurements. Temperature incubation, multiple shaking options, and reagent injectors with integrated heater and magnetic stirrer further enhance the system’s capabilities for complex workflows. Researchers can maintain cells under controlled conditions and perform real-time measurements without transferring plates between instruments. Simplified Workflow with Voyager Software To complement its hardware innovations, the VANTAstar comes with Voyager, BMG LABTECH’s intuitive control interface. Voyager is designed to remove complexity from assay setup, making data acquisition straightforward and efficient. Users can start measurements with minimal configuration, while the software automatically determines optimal settings for sensitivity and focus. Applications Across Life Sciences VANTAstar supports a broad spectrum of applications, including: Fluorescence and luminescence assays for signal detection and kinetic studies. Absorbance measurements for protein quantification, ELISA, and nucleic acid analysis. TR-FRET and fluorescence polarization for studying molecular interactions. Cell-based assays under controlled atmospheric conditions for physiological relevance. Its flexibility makes it suitable for life science research, providing reliable results for both routine and advanced experiments. Automation-Friendly Design The VANTAstar offers easy integration into robotic systems. Its reduced footprint, automation-friendly plate carrier, and multiple software interfaces ensure seamless compatibility with leading automation platforms. Home/ Categories/ Life Sciences/ Immunology Article Why Your Cyclosporiasis Diarrhea Could Be More Explosive Than Others Differences in how the immune system controls a cyclospora infection explains why some people experience mild symptoms while others develop severe disease. Written byAzadeh Nasuhidehnavi, PhD and Wesley Kufel, PharmDBrought to you byThe Conversation Aug 2, 2026 | 4 min read Save for Later Cyclosporiasis can cause severe gastrointestinal distress, including explosive diarrhea. Image credit:©iStock.com, wildpixel PDF version As you may have seen in news headlines or social media memes, there’s explosive diarrhea going around. Understanding how the immune system responds to the parasite that causes the unfortunate disease called cyclosporiasis can help explain why the infection is mild in some people but can cause severe or long-lasting illness in others.1 The 2026 outbreak of cyclosporiasis in the US has sickened over 4,100 people as of July. Public health officials have traced a major cluster of illness to contaminated produce imported from Mexico. As immunologists, we see cyclosporiasis as an interesting example of how the immune system influences disease. Unlike many bacteria that release toxins that damage body tissues, the parasite that causes cyclosporiasis infects the cells lining the small intestine and lives inside them. Symptoms of the infection, including explosive diarrhea, are caused mainly by the body’s immune response to the parasite. As a result, the severity and duration of infection depend largely on how well the immune system controls it. How Your Immune System Fights Cyclosporiasis Cyclosporiasis is caused by a microscopic parasite named Cyclospora cayetanensis. When a person ingests Cyclospora eggs by consuming contaminated food or water, the tough protective wall of the parasite breaks down in the gut and releases spores that can swim through your digestive tract. These spores swiftly invade the cells lining the small intestine, triggering a specialized branch of the immune system that targets threats inside cells. It takes time for Cyclospora to complete its life cycle and replicate enough for symptoms to start. CDC/Wikimedia Commons In healthy people, the key players in this immune response are helper T cells, which act as the commanders of the immune system. These cells release a powerful chemical alarm called interferon-gamma that instructs infected tissues to stop parasites inside other cells from multiplying.2 Interferon-gamma acts like an emergency pathway, raising the signal to deprive nutrients from parasites to starve them or use chemicals produced in the cell to kill them. Continue reading below... Like this story? Sign up for FREE Microbiology updates: Latest science news storiesTopic-tailored resources and eventsCustomized newsletter content Subscribe However, the intestinal lining needs time to heal and resolve inflammation even after the parasite has been eliminated.3 Depending on how long that takes, you could still experience symptoms of the illness for several weeks. Who is Most at Risk of Severe Infection? Because recovering from infection profoundly depends on an effective immune response, cyclosporiasis can be especially severe in young children, older adults, and people with weakened immune systems.4 Diarrh...

The article was published on 2026-08-24. The research on Wolbachia's role in controlling disease vectors spans over a century, with specific findings from recent studies.
Location and population
The article discusses global efforts, including the World Mosquito Program in Brazil and citizen science projects in various locations. It also references the use of Wolbachia in mosquitoes that transmit dengue and West Nile viruses.
Event
Researchers are using Wolbachia-infected mosquitoes to control the spread of mosquito-borne viruses such as dengue and chikungunya. The article also mentions the use of Wolbachia to target parasitic filarial worms and the development of drugs that inhibit Wolbachia's growth to combat these infections.
Scale, impact and risk
Wolbachia-infected mosquitoes have been used in Brazil to control disease spread. The article highlights the potential of Wolbachia to reduce the transmission of dengue and chikungunya viruses. It also notes that the bacteria can be used to target parasitic worms, which could have significant public health impacts.
Response, status and uncertainty
The use of Wolbachia-infected mosquitoes is an ongoing strategy, with the World Mosquito Program actively implementing it in Brazil. Research on the mechanism of Wolbachia's antiviral effects is ongoing, and citizen science projects are contributing to the understanding of Wolbachia's presence in various insect species.

Mosquito-Borne Diseases Infected Over 1 L In State From ’14 To ’24: Docs - The Times of India

Mosquito-Borne Diseases Infected Over 1 L In State From ’14 To ’24: Docs - The Times of India
新闻简报The article reports that mosquito-borne diseases, including dengue, malaria, and chikungunya, have infected over 1 lakh people in West Bengal from 2014 to 2024. Kolkata accounted for 20%-30% of the casualties, with the most severe dengue outbreak in 2023 resulting in over 13,000 cases and 25-40 reported deaths. While dengue and malaria have shown a decline in fatalities, chikungunya has caused joint-pain morbidities but no official deaths. Experts warn that the city’s weather and civic conditions still pose a risk for outbreaks. They emphasize the importance of prevention, awareness, and collective efforts to reduce mosquito breeding and transmission. The article highlights the need for sustained action to build mosquito-resilient communities, as noted in the World Mosquito Day 2026 theme. The report is based on official records and health department estimates, with some unofficial data suggesting higher numbers due to co-infections and classification delays.
查看新闻五要素
时间
该文章发表于2026-08-20。事件日期范围为2014年至2024年,其中在加尔各答发生的最后一次主要登革暴发疫情出现在2023年。
地点与对象
The report focuses on West Bengal, specifically Kolkata, where mosquito-borne diseases have infected over 1 lakh people in the state from 2014 to 2024. The affected population includes residents of Kolkata and the broader West Bengal region.
事件
Mosquito-borne diseases, including dengue, malaria, and chikungunya, have infected over 1 lakh people in West Bengal from 2014 to 2024. The event is reported by virologists and infectious diseases experts, and it is confirmed based on official records and health department estimates.
规模、影响与风险
Over 1 lakh infections were reported in the state from 2014 to 2024, with dengue being the most fatal. Kolkata accounted for 20%-30% of the casualties. The last major dengue outbreak in Kolkata in 2023 had over 13,000 cases and 25-40 reported deaths. Chikungunya, while causing joint-pain morbidities, has no official deaths recorded. The risk of outbreaks remains due to the city’s weather and civic conditions.
应对、状态与不确定性
专家警告称,尽管今年持续且强降雨、提高意识及有效市政措施有助于遏制蚊媒疾病传播,但该市气候和市政条件仍使其易受登革热疫情影响。应对措施持续进行,重点在于预防、提高意识及共同努力减少蚊虫孳生和传播。

According to them, these diseases have claimed over 400 lives in the state in the last decade. Kolkata accounted for 20%-30% of the casualties. Over a lakh were infected by dengue, malaria and chikungunya during the period in the city, making them the most fatal infectious diseases in the city.Consistent and heavier showers have probably helped curb the spread of these diseases this year, along with greater awareness and effective civic measures, said physicians. But the city’s weather and civic conditions still make it prone to an outbreak, they warned. “The number of dengue and malaria patients has been low this year,” said Nishant Agarwal, head of emergency at Charnock Hospital. Internal Medicine Consultant at Fortis Hospital Anandapur Joydeep Ghosh said dengue remains an important public health concern, and prevention is the most effective defence.An Aedes mosquito bite, which can transmit dengue, chikungunya or Zika, may appear as a small red bump accompanied by itching or mild swelling. by Taboolaby Taboola Sponsored LinksSponsored Links Promoted LinksPromoted Links You May Like Whole House Generators Prevent Outages Reliably. (Take A Look)TrendingSearch | Whole House Generator Learn More Undo “The real concern is the possibility of fever or flu-like symptoms,” said Ghosh. Dengue typically presents with sudden high fever, headache, muscle and joint pain, weakness and sometimes a rash, said Manipal Hospital infectious diseases physician Sayan Chakrabarty. Treatment is primarily supportive, with hydration and medical monitoring being important, said Ghosh.GRAPH“World Mosquito Day 2026, with the theme ‘Taking Action Together: Building Mosquito-Resilient Communities,’ highlights the need for collective and sustained efforts to prevent mosquito-borne diseases. Building resilient communities begins with individual awareness but requires families, neighbourhoods, healthcare professionals and local authorities to work together to reduce mosquito breeding and transmission.”Mosquito Day Resolutions:Every household, community and institution has a role to play.Eliminate breeding sites, protect ourselves from mosquito bites, recognise symptoms early and seek timely medical careWe can collectively create healthier and more mosquito-resilient communities.Prevention, vigilance and timely action can save lives.Casualties Over the Past Decade (2014–2024)Dengue: West Bengal recorded severe annual spikes, with total official state deaths ranging between 200 and 400+ overall across the decade. During peak years, official annual state death tolls ranged from 30 to 46+ (e.g., 46 deaths in 2017, 30 in 2022), though unofficial estimate reports regularly cited higher numbers due to co-infections and classification delays. Of the state totals, Kolkata accounted for roughly 20–30% of total casualties.Malaria: Malaria-related fatalities dropped significantly across the state, with official deaths remaining in the single digits or near zero annually. Kolkata reports thousands of Plasmodium vivax and falciparum infections annually, but fatal cases remain extremely rare.Chikungunya: While sporadic outbreaks and joint-pain morbidities occur throughout Kolkata and Bengal, official records register 0 deaths from chikungunya, as the virus is non-fatal.Last Major Dengue Outbreak in KolkataYear: The most severe outbreak in Kolkata occurred in 2023 (surpassing the previous major outbreak in 2022).Casualties & Tally:Across West Bengal, official and health department estimates recorded 90,000 to 100,000+ total infections and over 100 casualties statewide.In Kolkata alone, total dengue infections surpassed 13,000 cases, with over 25 to 40 reported deaths within the city limits and immediate metropolitan suburbs. 面对登革热疫情激增,专家呼吁提升公众意识与加强中央监管——daily-sun.com

Calls for wider awareness, central oversight amid dengue surge - daily-sun.com
新闻简报报道称孟加拉国登革热病例激增,并指出包括达卡南市政公司(DSCC)和达卡北市政公司(DNCC)在内的市政机构未能在世界蚊子日开展协调行动。据孟加拉国卫生服务总局(DGHS)数据,截至2026年8月19日早8时的24小时内,至少2名患者死亡、671人住院,使得该年度累计死亡人数达76例、住院人数达26,272例。公共卫生专家(包括GM Saifur Rahman博士)批评当局在媒介防控方面采取碎片化、被动应对的方式,并指出技术专长、后勤保障及专业人员短缺问题严重。专家强调需在全国范围内开展监测研究与结构化预防项目,并建立中央监管机制以应对日益严峻的危机。报道指出孟加拉国登革热管理体系存在系统性缺陷,呼吁加强公共卫生参与以挽救生命。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该报道发布于2026年8月19日,最新登革热数据报告时间为当日早8时。报道未明确指出具体事件日期,但登革热病例激增仍在持续。
地点与对象
事件主要发生在孟加拉国,具体涉及达卡南市政公司(DSCC)和达卡北市政公司(DNCC)。受影响人群包括城市与农村地区,病例已扩散至农村联合委员会。
事件
孟加拉国正经历登革热病例激增,公共卫生专家呼吁提升公众意识并加强中央监管。尽管当前危机持续,达卡市政机构未组织任何活动纪念世界蚊子日。
规模、影响与风险
据DGHS数据,截至2026年8月19日早8时的24小时内,至少2名患者死亡、671人住院。该年度登革热累计死亡76例、住院26,272例。当前风险在于防控措施碎片化、被动应对,缺乏有效的媒介防控体系。
应对、状态与不确定性
公共卫生专家与昆虫学家批评当局因技术专长、后勤保障及专业人员短缺而未能有效控制蚊媒种群。目前尚无统一的中央机构负责整合媒介管理,各市政机构独立运作。局势未获解决,亦无结构化预防项目实施。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Calls for wider awareness, central oversight amid dengue surge - daily-sun.com

News Brief
The article reports on a surge in dengue cases in Bangladesh, highlighting the lack of coordinated efforts by city corporations like Dhaka South and North City Corporations to mark World Mosquito Day. According to the Directorate General of Health Services (DGHS), at least two patients died and 671 were hospitalized in the 24 hours leading up to 19 August 2026, bringing the total annual toll to 76 deaths and 26,272 hospitalizations. Public health experts, including Dr. GM Saifur Rahman, criticize the fragmented and reactive approach to vector control, noting acute shortages in technical expertise, logistics, and trained personnel. They emphasize the need for nationwide surveillance, research, and structured preventive programs, as well as central oversight to address the growing crisis. The article underscores the systemic flaws in Bangladesh's dengue management and calls for improved public health engagement to save lives.
Original source text (English)
Latest Bangladesh Business World Sports Opinion Politics Diplomacy More E-Paper ☀️ 🌐 English বাংলা Russian Hindi Chinese Trending Rangpur Quadruple Murder Mirza Fakhrul Gold Price Tarique Rahman US-Iran Talks × Today's Newspaper Latest World Cup Magazine 2026 Election 2026 Follow Us Print: 25 Aug 2026 Today's Newspaper WORLD MOSQUITO DAY Calls for wider awareness, central oversight amid dengue surge Daily Sun Report, Dhaka Published: 20 Aug 2026, 12:00 AM Updated: 19 Aug 2026, 11:39 PM Share Share options Print Copy link Facebook X WhatsApp Linkedin A A Bangladesh marks World Mosquito Day today amidst an ongoing surge in dengue cases, with public health experts emphasising the need for wider community outreach and stronger institutional coordination. Observed globally every year on 20 August, the day highlights the world’s deadliest animal and the array of vector-borne illnesses it transmits, including dengue, Zika, chikungunya, and yellow fever, which continue to threaten billions worldwide. Yet, as hospital admissions and fatalities from dengue climb daily across Bangladesh, both the Dhaka South City Corporation (DSCC) and Dhaka North City Corporation (DNCC) have planned no programmes or public engagements to mark the occasion. When contacted, public relations officers from both city bodies confirmed the absence of any official events. DSCC Public Relations Officer Rasel Rahman and DNCC Public Relations Officer Zakir Hossain both conceded that an event should have been scheduled, but confirmed none had been organised. The inaction comes against the backdrop of a steadily worsening dengue crisis. According to Directorate General of Health Services (DGHS) data released on Wednesday morning, at least two more patients died and 671 were hospitalised across the country in the 24 hours to 8am on 19 August. The country’s total Dengue toll for the year now stands at 76 deaths and 26,272 hospitalisations. Public health experts and entomologists have criticized the authorities' handling of the vector, noting that despite allocating millions of takas for larvicides and adulticides, city corporations have consistently failed to curb mosquito populations due to acute shortages in technical expertise, logistics, and trained personnel. Dr GM Saifur Rahman, a medical entomologist and associate professor at National University, stressed that World Mosquito Day should serve as a key platform for public health engagement. “This day is directly tied to public safety – mosquitoes affect everyone, and people are dying,” Dr Rahman said. “We should be hosting rallies, discussions, essay competitions, and art events in educational institutions to educate youth and civil society. Crucially, we also need professional, scientific symposia on vector control. Yet, those responsible have made no effort.” Saifur Rahman highlighted fundamental systemic flaws in Bangladesh's vector control apparatus, warning that efforts remain fragmented and reactive. “There is no central body overseeing integrated vector management across the country. City corporations and municipalities operate in isolation. We are now seeing dengue cases emerging from rural union parishads, where there is virtually no manpower or equipment to fight mosquitoes. Nationwide surveillance, vector research, and structured preventive programmes remain virtually non-existent,” he added, calling for meaningful observation of the day to drive evidence-based dengue control and save lives. 海得拉巴因持续降雨和高湿度出现季节性病毒热及流感样疾病激增——《Telangana Today》

Hyderabad sees seasonal surge in viral fevers, flu-like illnesses amid wet weather - Telangana Today
新闻简报印度海得拉巴因持续降雨和高湿度,正出现季节性病毒热及流感样疾病激增,相关情况已由《Telangana Today》于2026年8月19日报道。此次激增与媒介媒介传播疾病相关,其症状与登革热及基孔肯雅热存在重叠。多位医生指出,许多患者登革热及疟疾检测结果为阴性,表明真正的致病原可能为高致病性病毒株及呼吸道病原体。主要医院及诊所的门诊部接诊量显著上升。公共卫生当局已敦促居民采取预防措施,包括清除积水及避免自行用药,以防止疾病传播。当前疫情仍在调查中,尚未报告具体病例或死亡人数。此次激增为季节性模式的一部分,未确认存在暴发特定病原体。
查看新闻五要素
时间
持续降雨和高湿度导致海得拉巴在雨季出现病毒热及流感样疾病的季节性上升。相关情况已于2026年8月19日发布。
地点与对象
此次事件发生在印度海得拉巴(特伦甘纳邦),涉及普通人群,特别是经济弱势家庭,当局敦促其采取措施预防蚊媒孳生。
事件
海得拉巴正经历病毒热及流感样疾病的季节性激增,主要归因于持续降雨和高湿度。医生指出其症状与登革热及基孔肯雅热存在重叠,但具体病因尚未确认。相关情况由《Telangana Today》报道。
规模、影响与风险
海得拉巴主要医院及诊所门诊部接诊量激增,患者多表现出典型病毒热症状。卫生当局强调需警惕季节性疾病,但未提供具体病例或死亡人数。风险与媒介媒介传播疾病的传播相关,但未明确说明具体风险等级。
应对、状态与不确定性
公共卫生当局已敦促居民采取预防措施,包括清除积水孳生地、确保饮用水安全及避免自行用药。然而,当前疫情仍在调查中,具体暴发病原体尚未完全确认。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Hyderabad sees seasonal surge in viral fevers, flu-like illnesses amid wet weather - Telangana Today

News Brief
Hyderabad, India, is experiencing a seasonal surge in viral fevers and flu-like illnesses due to persistent wet weather and high humidity, as reported by Telangana Today on August 19, 2026. The outbreak is linked to vector-borne diseases, with symptoms overlapping those of dengue and chikungunya. Doctors have noted that many patients test negative for dengue and malaria, suggesting the real culprits are aggressive viral and respiratory strains. Outpatient departments in major hospitals and clinics are seeing an increase in patient walk-ins. Public health authorities have urged residents to take precautions, including eliminating stagnant water and avoiding self-medication, to prevent the spread of these illnesses. The situation remains under investigation, and no specific case or death counts have been reported. The surge is part of a broader seasonal pattern, with no confirmed outbreak of chikungunya virus itself.
Original source text (English)
Monday, Aug 24, 2026 Search Hyderabad Telangana AP News India World Entertainment Sport Science and Tech Business Rewind ... NRI View Point cartoon My Space Education Today Reviews Property Lifestyle E-Paper NRI View Point cartoon My Space Reviews Education Today Property Lifestyle Home | Hyderabad | Hyderabad Sees Seasonal Surge In Viral Fevers Flu Like Illnesses Amid Wet Weather Hyderabad sees seasonal surge in viral fevers, flu-like illnesses amid wet weather Persistent wet weather and high humidity have led to a seasonal rise in viral fevers and influenza-like illnesses in Hyderabad. Doctors say symptoms can overlap with dengue and chikungunya, while urging residents to prevent mosquito breeding and avoid self-medication. Exploring Guided Local City Tours By Telangana Today Published Date - 19 August 2026, 05:26 PM Hyderabad: Persistent wet weather and high humidity across Hyderabad have triggered a sharp seasonal surge in viral fevers, influenza-like illnesses (ILI), and suspected vector-borne cases. Outpatient departments across major State-run hospitals, including Fever Hospital, Nallakunta, Gandhi Hospital, Secunderabad, Basthi Dawakhanas, private nursing homes and clinics, are witnessing a surge in patient walk-ins with typical viral fever symptoms. Seasonal health tips According to senior doctors, a majority of patients are reporting overlapping symptoms, which means they are having identical warning symptoms for influenza, common cold, dengue and chikungunya. Powered By VidCrunch Powered by VidCrunch Next Stay Playback speed 1x Normal Quality Auto Back 322p 240p 144p Auto Back 0.25x 0.5x 1x Normal 1.5x 2x 00:00/01:00 The typical symptoms that patients at health care facilities are reporting include sudden high-grade fever, severe body aches and joint pain, extreme fatigue and exhaustion, and headaches. Ordering At-Home Infectious Disease Tests “Wet conditions play a big role in triggering viral fevers during the rainy season. It’s mostly water-borne and vector-borne ailments. Families residing in economically weaker section regions must take precautions from mosquitoes, which are the main culprit for diseases during this time of the year. We are witnessing a slight surge in viral fevers, but the important thing is that families can remain safe by taking simple precautions,” says Superintendent, Fever Hospital, Dr Musa Khan. Doctors have pointed out that prevailing clinical presentation typically includes sudden high-grade fevers, severe body aches (myalgia), extreme fatigue, and respiratory discomfort such as scratchy throats and persistent coughs. While public anxiety frequently centers around dengue during wet spells, doctors made it clear that a large number of patients test negative for dengue and malaria. The real culprits are the aggressive circulating viral and respiratory strains, they added. Managing Chronic Health Conditions Public health authorities have urged residents to remain vigilant against seasonal diseases by getting rid of stagnant water breeding sites, ensuring safe drinking water, and avoiding self-medication. 登革热、疟疾风险可能延续至雨季后,医生发出警告——《德干纪事报》

Dengue, Malaria Risk Can Continue Beyond Monsoon, Doctors Warn - Deccan Chronicle
新闻简报本文指出,蚊媒传染病如登革热、疟疾和基孔肯雅热不再严格局限于雨季。印度特伦甘纳邦的医生警告称,气候模式变化、气温上升及水资源管理不善已在全年营造出蚊虫滋生环境。他们强调持续开展蚊虫防控的重要性,包括清除积水和采取防蚊叮咬措施。文章作为一项警示,指出持续发热、反复呕吐及异常出血等症状不容忽视。然而,文中未报告确诊的暴发或具体病例/死亡人数。当前形势被描述为持续性的,且取决于当地环境因素而非季节性模式。
查看新闻五要素
时间
20 August 2026 5:22 PM, 24 August 2026.
地点与对象
印度特伦甘纳邦;城市地区内存在积水源头
事件
医生警告称,由于气候模式变化及水资源管理不善,蚊媒传染病如登革热、疟疾和基孔肯雅热可能在雨季后持续存在。此为一项警示与建议,而非确诊的暴发。
规模、影响与风险
未报告具体病例或死亡人数。风险被描述为取决于当地环境条件而非季节性模式。
应对、状态与不确定性
医生强调需全年开展蚊虫防控措施,包括清除积水及预防蚊虫叮咬。当前形势仍在讨论与建议中,未报告确诊的暴发或调查状态。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Dengue, Malaria Risk Can Continue Beyond Monsoon, Doctors Warn - Deccan Chronicle

News Brief
The article highlights that mosquito-borne diseases such as dengue, malaria, and chikungunya are no longer strictly confined to the monsoon season. Doctors in Telangana, India, warn that changing weather patterns, rising temperatures, and poor water management have created breeding grounds for mosquitoes throughout the year. They stress the importance of continuous mosquito control efforts, including removing stagnant water and using preventive measures against bites. The article serves as an advisory, noting that symptoms like persistent fever, repeated vomiting, and unusual bleeding should not be ignored. However, no confirmed outbreak or specific case/death counts are reported. The situation is described as ongoing and dependent on local environmental factors rather than calendar seasons.

Risk Can Continue Beyond Monsoon, Doctors Warn Telangana Deepika Pasham 20 August 2026 5:22 PM20 August 2026 5:22 PM Internal medicine specialist Dr Sudhir B.S. said changing weather conditions and poor water management were creating mosquito breeding sites throughout the year. x In cities, even small amounts of stagnant water in coolers, flowerpots, open containers, construction sites and terraces can provide breeding grounds.(File Photo) Select Deccan Chronicle Now Hyderabad: Mosquito-borne diseases such as dengue, malaria and chikungunya are no longer confined to the monsoon season, with changing weather patterns, rising temperatures, irregular rainfall and rapid urbanisation allowing mosquitoes to breed for longer periods, doctors said on the occasion of the World Mosquito Day. Senior general physician Dr Syed Mustafa Ashraf said mosquito-borne infections were increasingly being seen outside the traditional seasonal pattern. In cities, even small amounts of stagnant water in coolers, flowerpots, open containers, construction sites and terraces can provide breeding grounds. Also Read - Call For 75 Per Cent Reservations In Jobs For Adivasis Of Agency Areas In Police Recruitment Advertisement “Mosquito control cannot be limited to the monsoon. Prevention has to continue throughout the year,” he said, stressing the need to remove stagnant water, cover water containers and use measures to prevent mosquito bites. Senior consultant, general medicine, Dr Chaitanya Challa said one of the major concerns was that people often dismiss fever outside the monsoon as a routine viral infection. Dengue, malaria and chikungunya can initially cause similar symptoms such as fever, headache, body pain, weakness, nausea and vomiting. Also Read - Hyd-Based Tech Start-Up Water Raises $2.5 Million “Persistent fever, repeated vomiting, abdominal pain, unusual bleeding, excessive weakness, drowsiness or breathing difficulty should not be ignored,” he said. He cautioned that in dengue, a fall in temperature does not always mean that the patient is recovering. The critical phase can occur around the time the fever settles, usually during days three to seven of illness and some patients can develop complications during this period. Also Read - Special Drive To Complete 16.06 Saplings Planting From Today Discover more Implementing Enterprise AI And Machine Learning Tools E-paper subscription OTT Platform Guide Senior consultant-general physician Dr Krishna Murthy Daram said the risk of mosquito-borne diseases depended on local temperature, rainfall, humidity, mosquito activity and environmental conditions rather than simply the calendar season. Internal medicine specialist Dr Sudhir B.S. said changing weather conditions and poor water management were creating mosquito breeding sites throughout the year. Time
20 August 2026 5:22 PM, 24 August 2026.
Location and population
Telangana, India; urban areas with stagnant water sources.
Event
Doctors warn that mosquito-borne diseases like dengue, malaria, and chikungunya can persist beyond the monsoon season due to changing weather patterns and poor water management. This is a warning and advisory, not a confirmed outbreak.
Scale, impact and risk
No specific case or death counts are reported. The risk is described as dependent on local environmental conditions rather than seasonal patterns.
Response, status and uncertainty
Doctors emphasize the need for year-round mosquito control measures, including removing stagnant water and preventing mosquito bites. The situation is under ongoing discussion and advisory, with no confirmed outbreak or investigation status.
